# Palestinians

"Palestinian" redirects here. For other uses, see [Palestinian (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Philistines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philistines).

**Palestinians** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الفلسطينيون, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *al-Filasṭīniyyūn*) are an [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity) [national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) group native to the region of [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)), descended from those who have inhabited the area over the millennia.[^cite_note-indigenous-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-palestineeb-35] They represent a highly homogenized community who share a [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_culture) and [ethnic identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_identity),[^cite_note-36] and close [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabic) and cultural ties with other [Levantine Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic).&#x20;

In 1919, [Palestinian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Palestine) and [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) constituted 90 percent of the population of Palestine, just before the [third wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Aliyah) of [Jewish immigration](351513.md) and the setting up of British [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) after [World War I](4764461.md).[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] Opposition to Jewish immigration spurred the consolidation of [a unified national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_identity), though Palestinian society was still fragmented by regional, class, religious, and family differences.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] The history of the Palestinian national identity is a disputed issue amongst scholars.[^cite_note-Likhovski-41][^cite_note-Gelvin2014-42] For some, the term "[Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definitions_of_Palestinian)" is used to refer to the nationalist concept of a Palestinian people by Palestinian Arabs from the late 19th century and in the pre–World War I period, while others assert the Palestinian identity encompasses the heritage of all eras from pre-[biblical times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ancient_Israel_and_Judah) up to the [Ottoman period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria).[^cite_note-palestineeb-35][^cite_note-Lewis-43][^cite_note-Khalidip18-44] After the [Israeli Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence), the [1948 Palestinian expulsion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight), and more so after the [1967 Palestinian exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Palestinian_exodus), the term "Palestinian" evolved into a sense of a shared future in the form of aspirations for a [Palestinian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_State_of_Palestine).[^cite_note-palestineeb-35] Though the concept of Palestinian citizenship for the purpose of international law has been revived, the pending [realization of self-determination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_self-determination) is still insufficient, thus Palestinians remain over the threshold of eligibility to receive international protection as refugees and stateless persons.[^cite_note-45]&#x20;

Founded in 1964, the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) is an umbrella organization for groups that represent the Palestinian people before international states.[^cite_note-IMEU-46] The [Palestinian National Authority](24093.md), officially established in 1994 as a result of the [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords), is an interim administrative body nominally responsible for governance in Palestinian population centres in the West Bank and the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-47] Since 1978, the [United Nations](31769.md) has observed an annual [International Day of Solidarity with the Palestinian People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Day_of_Solidarity_with_the_Palestinian_People).&#x20;

Despite various [wars](7960202.md) and [exoduses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_exodus_\(disambiguation\)), roughly one half of the world's Palestinian population continues to reside in the territory of former [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), now encompassing [Israel](9282173.md) and the [occupied](2781576.md) [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories) of the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md).[^cite_note-Ember2005-48] In Israel proper, Palestinians constitute almost 21 percent of the population as part of its [Arab citizens](492331.md).[^cite_note-critical-49] Many are [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) or [internally displaced Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Present_absentee), including over 1.4 million in the Gaza Strip,[^cite_note-WWWGazaStrip-2] over 870,000 in the West Bank,[^cite_note-WWWWestBank-50] and around 250,000 in Israel proper. Of the Palestinian population who live abroad, known as the [Palestinian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_diaspora), more than half are [stateless](789786.md), lacking legal citizenship in any country.[^cite_note-51] Of the diaspora population, 2.3 million are registered as refugees in neighboring [Jordan](7515964.md), most [of whom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinians_in_Jordan) hold Jordanian citizenship;[^cite_note-unjo-6][^cite_note-PCBSJordan-52] over 1 million live between [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md), and about 750,000 live in [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), with [Chile](5489.md) holding [the largest Palestinian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinians_in_Chile) concentration (around half a million) outside of the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world).&#x20;

## Etymology

See also: [Timeline of the name *Palestine*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_name_Palestine)

The [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) toponym *Palaistínē* (Παλαιστίνη), which is the origin of the [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) *Filasṭīn* (فلسطين), first occurs in the work of the 5th century BCE [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) historian [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus), where it denotes generally[^cite_note-Exception-53] the coastal land from [Phoenicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) down to [Egypt](8087628.md).[^cite_note-Herodotus1-54][^cite_note-Cohenp36-55] Herodotus also employs the term as an [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym), as when he speaks of the "Syrians of Palestine" or "Palestinian-Syrians",[^cite_note-Herodotus2-56] an ethnically amorphous group he distinguishes from the Phoenicians.[^cite_note-Kasher-57][^cite_note-58] Herodotus makes no distinction between the inhabitants of Palestine.[^cite_note-59]&#x20;



1650s [maps of the region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartography_of_Palestine) by Ottoman geographer [Kâtip Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%A2tip_%C3%87elebi), showing the term أرض فلسطين ("Land of Palestine")

The Greek word reflects an ancient Eastern Mediterranean-Near Eastern word which was used either as a [toponym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponym) or [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym). In [Ancient Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages) *Peleset/Purusati*[^cite_note-strange159-60] has been conjectured to refer to the "[Sea Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Peoples)", particularly the [Philistines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philistines).[^cite_note-AK2013-61][^cite_note-Drews48-62] Among [Semitic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages), [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) *Palaštu* (variant *Pilištu*) is used of 7th-century Philistia and its, by then, four city states.[^cite_note-63] [Biblical Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew)'s cognate word *Plištim*, is usually translated Philistines.[^cite_note-strange159-60]&#x20;

When the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) conquered the region in the first century BCE, they used the name [Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)) for the province that covered most of the region. At the same time, the name *Syria Palestina* continued to be used by historians and geographers to refer to the area between the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) and the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River), as in the writings of [Philo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philo), [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) and [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder). During the early 2nd century CE, [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina) became the official administrative name in a move viewed by scholars as an attempt by emperor [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian) to disassociate Jews from the land as punishment for the [Bar Kokhba revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar_Kokhba_revolt).[^cite_note-H.H._Ben-Sasson,_1976,_page_334-64][^cite_note-Ariel_Lewin_p._33-65][^cite_note-F90-66] Jacobson suggested the change to be rationalized by the fact that the new province was far larger.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobson200144–45-67] The name was thenceforth inscribed on coins, and beginning in the fifth century, mentioned in [rabbinic texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_literature).[^cite_note-H.H._Ben-Sasson,_1976,_page_334-64][^cite_note-Cohenp37-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldman1996553-69] The Arabic word *Filastin* has been used to refer to the region since the time of the earliest [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval) Arab [geographers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographer). It appears to have been used as an [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) [adjectival noun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisba_\(suffix\)) in the region since as early as the 7th century (see for example [Al-Maqdisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Maqdisi) and [Mujir al-Din](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujir_al-Din)).[^cite_note-Kishp200-70]&#x20;



[Khalil Beidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalil_Beidas) (1874–1949) whose writings played an important role in the development of Palestinian nationalist consciousness

In modern times, the first person to self-describe Palestine's Arabs as "Palestinians" was [Khalil Beidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalil_Beidas) in 1898, followed by Salim Quba'in and [Najib Nassar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najib_Nassar) in 1902. After the 1908 [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution), which eased press censorship laws in the Ottoman Empire, dozens of newspapers and periodicals were founded in Palestine, and the term "Palestinian" expanded in usage. Among those were the *Al-Quds*, *Al-Munadi*, *[Falastin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falastin_\(newspaper\))*, *[Al-Karmil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Karmil)* and Al-Nafir newspapers, which used the term "Filastini" more than 170 times in 110 articles from 1908 to 1914. They also made references to a "Palestinian society", "Palestinian nation", and a "Palestinian diaspora". Article writers included Christian and Muslim Arab Palestinians, Palestinian emigrants, and non-Palestinian Arabs.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] The Palestinian Arab Christian newspaper Falastin had addressed its readers as Palestinians since its inception in 1911 during the Ottoman period.[^cite_note-AAP1-73][^cite_note-APP2-74]&#x20;

During the [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) period, the term "Palestinian" was used to refer to all people residing there, regardless of religion or [ethnicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity), and those granted [citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship) by the British Mandatory authorities were granted "Palestinian citizenship".[^cite_note-75] Other examples include the use of the term [Palestine Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Regiment) to refer to the Jewish Infantry Brigade Group of the British Army during World War II, and the term "Palestinian Talmud", which is an alternative name of the [Jerusalem Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Talmud), used mainly in academic sources.&#x20;



1936 issue of the Palestinian Arab Christian *[Falastin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falastin_\(newspaper\))* newspaper addressed its readers as "Palestinians" since its establishment in 1911.

[^cite_note-AAP1-73]

Following the 1948 [establishment of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence), the use and application of the terms "Palestine" and "Palestinian" by and to [Palestinian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Jews) largely dropped from use. For example, the English-language newspaper *[The Palestine Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Palestine_Post)*, founded by Jews in 1932, changed its name in 1950 to *[The Jerusalem Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jerusalem_Post)*. The term [Arab Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Jews) can include Jews with Palestinian heritage and Israeli citizenship, although some Arab Jews prefer to be called [Mizrahi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews). Non-Jewish [Arab citizens of Israel](492331.md) with Palestinian heritage identify themselves as Arabs or Palestinians.[^cite_note-Kershner-76] These non-Jewish Arab Israelis thus include those that are Palestinian by heritage but Israeli by citizenship.[^cite_note-CFR-Kurlantzick-77]&#x20;

The [Palestinian National Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Charter), as amended by the PLO's [Palestinian National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Council) in July 1968, defined "Palestinians" as "those Arab nationals who, until 1947, normally resided in Palestine regardless of whether they were evicted from it or stayed there. Anyone born, after that date, of a Palestinian father – whether in Palestine or outside it – is also a Palestinian."[^cite_note-charter-78] Note that "Arab nationals" is *not* religious-specific, and it includes not only the Arabic-speaking Muslims of Palestine but also the [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) and other religious communities of Palestine who were at that time Arabic-speakers, such as the [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) and [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze). Thus, the [Jews of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Jews) were/are also included, although limited only to "the [\[Arabic-speaking\] Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Jews) who had normally resided in Palestine until the beginning of the \[pre-state] [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist) invasion." The Charter also states that "Palestine with the [boundaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border) it had during the British Mandate, is an indivisible territorial unit."[^cite_note-charter-78][^cite_note-Draft-79]&#x20;

## Origins

Further information: [Demographic history of Palestine (region)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_history_of_Palestine_\(region\))

Historic records and genetic studies suggest that the Palestinian people are descendants of ancient Levantine populations.[^cite_note-Nebel20002-80][^cite_note-Agranat22-81][^cite_note-pmid2056020522-82][^cite_note-\:1122-83][^cite_note-dasr22-84][^cite_note-levant-dna22-85][^cite_note-86] Palestinians are sometimes described as [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples).[^cite_note-indigenous-32] According to [International Work Group for Indigenous Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Work_Group_for_Indigenous_Affairs), the "Indigenous Peoples of Palestine are the Bedouin Jahalin, al-Kaabneh, al-Azazmeh, al-Ramadin and al-Rshaida".[^cite_note-87]&#x20;



Palestinian mother and child, circa 1920

Palestine has undergone many demographic and religious upheavals throughout history. During the [2nd millennium BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_millennium_BC), it was inhabited by the [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan#Canaanites), [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages)-speaking peoples who practiced the [Canaanite religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_religion).[^cite_note-88] Most Palestinians share a strong genetic link to the ancient Canaanites.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) later emerged as an outgrowth of southern [Canaanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan) civilization, with [Jews](25955086.md) and [Israelite Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) eventually forming the majority of the population in Palestine during [classical antiquity](252905.md),[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] However, the Jewish population in [Jerusalem](16043.md) and its surroundings in [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea), and Samaritan population in [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria), never fully recovered as a result of the [Jewish-Roman Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Roman_wars) and [Samaritan revolts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritan_revolts) respectively.[^cite_note-97]&#x20;

In the centuries that followed, the region experienced [political and economic unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_of_the_Third_Century) and the [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_persecution) of minorities.[^cite_note-Kessler2010-98][^cite_note-99] Mass conversions to [Christianity](5211.md) (and the subsequent [Christianization of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_Christianization_of_the_Roman_Empire)), along with the emigration of [pagans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism), Jews, and Samaritans, contributed to a Christian majority forming in [Late Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina) and [Byzantine Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_the_East).[^cite_note-CHJ2-100][^cite_note-\:5-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-\:142-103]&#x20;

In the 7th century, the Arab [Rashiduns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) [conquered the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant); they were later succeeded by other Arab [Muslim](19541.md) dynasties, including the [Umayyads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) and the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate).[^cite_note-104] Over the following several centuries, the population of Palestine drastically decreased, from an estimated 1 million during the Roman and Byzantine periods to about 300,000 by the early Ottoman period.[^cite_note-\:Broshi1979-105][^cite_note-\:4-106] Over time, the existing population adopted Arab culture and language and much [converted to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization).[^cite_note-\:5-101] The settlement of Arabs before and after the Muslim conquest is thought to have played a role in accelerating the Islamization process.[^cite_note-\:2-107][^cite_note-\:Ellenblum2010-108][^cite_note-109][^cite_note-\:3-110] Some scholars suggest that by the arrival of the [Crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades), Palestine was already overwhelmingly Muslim,[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-Tessler-112] while others claim that it was only after the Crusades that the Christians lost their majority, and that the process of mass Islamization took place much later, perhaps during the [Mamluk period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate).[^cite_note-\:2-107][^cite_note-113] According to Palestinian historian [Nazmi Al-Ju'beh](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Nazmi_Al-Ju%27beh\&action=edit\&redlink=1), as with in other Arab nations, the [Arab identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity) of Palestinians is largely based on [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) and [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture) affiliation, and is independent of the existence of any actual Arabian origins.[^cite_note-114]&#x20;

For several centuries during the [Ottoman period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Syria) the population in Palestine declined and fluctuated between 150,000 and 250,000 inhabitants, and it was only in the 19th century that a rapid population growth began to occur.[^cite_note-115] This growth was aided by the immigration of [Egyptians](31912046.md) (during the reigns of [Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt) and [Ibrahim Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Pasha_of_Egypt)) and [Algerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerians) (following the [French conquest of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Algeria)) in the first half of the 19th century, and the subsequent immigration of Algerians, [Bosniaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosniaks), and [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) during the second half of the century.[^cite_note-\:63-116][^cite_note-\:FrantzmanKark20133-117] Between 1871 and 1945, around a dozen villages were established by immigrants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrantzmanKark201389-118]&#x20;

Many Palestinian villagers claim ancestral ties to [Arab tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) from the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) that [settled in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Levant) during or after the [Muslim conquest of the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant).[^cite_note-\:73-119] Some Palestinian families, notably in the [Hebron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebron_Hills) and [Nablus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_Nablus) regions, claim Jewish and [Samaritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) ancestry respectively, preserving associated cultural customs and traditions.[^cite_note-LS201022-120][^cite_note-Hajjah20212-121][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBen_Zvi19858-122]&#x20;

## Genetics

Further information: [Genetic history of the Middle East § Israel and Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_the_Middle_East#Israel_and_Palestine)



In a whole-genome study of modern-day ethnic groups in the world, Palestinian samples clustered in the "Middle Eastern genomic group" (shown as "GG5" in left image), which included samples from populations such as [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans), [Bedouins](61770.md), [Jordanians](7515964.md), [Iraqi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Jew) and [Yemenite Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenite_Jews). Left image shows [clustering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluster_analysis) following [principal component analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_component_analysis) with [three dimensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-dimensional_space). Top right image shows geographical locations where samples were collected.

[^cite_note-Kim_Choi_Kim_2023-123]

Genetic studies indicate that the Palestinian people descend mostly from ancient Levantines extending back to [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age_Levant) inhabitants of the Levant.[^cite_note-jordantimes.com-16][^cite_note-124][^cite_note-\:0-125][^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-\:1-128][^cite_note-\:22-129] They also note a genetic relationship between Palestinians and other [Arab](2185.md) and [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_people) groups in the [Middle East and North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_and_North_Africa)[^cite_note-\:1122-83][^cite_note-Behar20102-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] as well as a genetic relationship between Palestinians and various jewish groups.[^cite_note-Nebel20002-80][^cite_note-pmid111539182-133][^cite_note-pmid2056020522-82]&#x20;

**Levantine origins**&#x20;





[Principal component analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_component_analysis). Top image Non-gray colors show ancient samples above gray-colored modern populations, whereas bottom image shows the labeled modern-day populations beneath. Palestinians cluster with Bronze Age Levantine samples, The authors of the study concluded that the "overlap between the Bronze Age and present-day Levantines suggests a degree of genetic continuity in the region." (Haber et al., 2017)

A 2015 study by Verónica Fernandes and others concluded that Palestinians have a "primarily indigenous origin".[^cite_note-134]&#x20;

In a 2016 study by Scarlett Marshall and others published in [Nature](43427.md), the study concluded that the biogeographical affinities of "both Syrians and Palestinians are highly localised to the Levant", the authors also noted that the biogeographical affinity of Palestinians goes in agreement with historical records and previous studies on their [uniparental markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniparental_marker), which all suggest that Palestinians at least in part descend from local [Israelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) converts to Islam after the [Islamic expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_expansion).[^cite_note-\:32-135]&#x20;

According to a study published in June 2017 by [Ranajit Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ranajit_Das\&action=edit\&redlink=1), [Paul Wexler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Wexler_\(linguist\)), [Mehdi Pirooznia](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mehdi_Pirooznia\&action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Eran Elhaik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eran_Elhaik) in *[Frontiers in Genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontiers_in_Genetics)*, in a [principal component analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal_component_analysis), Natufians, together with a Neolithic Levantine sample, "clustered predominantly with modern-day Palestinians" and that Palestinians have a "predominant" ancient Levantine origin and residual Iranian origin, with some [Eastern Hunter-Gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Hunter-Gatherer) and smaller amounts of [Anatolian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_Neolithic) admixture.[^cite_note-\:1-128]&#x20;

In a study published in August 2017 by Marc Haber et al. in *The American Journal of Human Genetics*, the authors concluded that: "The overlap between the Bronze Age and present-day Levantines suggests a degree of genetic continuity in the region".[^cite_note-136] later in 2024 Haber also commented that all modern [Levantine Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic) descend from Canaanite-like ancestors, and later migrations' impact on their population ancestry was slight.[^cite_note-137]&#x20;

A 2020 study on human remains from [Middle Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Bronze_Age) [Southern Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Levant)([2100–1550](tel\:2100%E2%80%931550) BC) populations suggests a significant degree of genetic continuity in Arabic-speaking Levantine populations, as well as several Jewish groups. Palestinians, among other Levantine groups, were found to derive 81–87% of their ancestry from Bronze age Levantines, relating to [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanites) as well as [Kura–Araxes culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura%E2%80%93Araxes_culture) impact from before 2400 BCE (4400 [years before present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Years_before_present)); 8–12% from an [East African](260987.md) source and 5–10% from Bronze age [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europeans). Results show that a significant European component was added to the region since the Bronze Age (on average \~8.7%), seemingly related to the [Sea Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Peoples), excluding Ashkenazi and Moroccan Jews who harbour greater European-related ancestry, both populations having a history in Europe.[^cite_note-\:0-125]&#x20;

A 2021 study by the [New York Genome Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Genome_Center) found that the predominant component of the DNA of modern Palestinians matches that of Bronze Age [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanites) who lived around 2500–1700 BCE.[^cite_note-\:22-129]&#x20;

A 2023 study, which looked at the [whole genomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_genome_sequencing) of modern-day ethnic groups around the world, found that the Palestinian samples clustered in the "Middle Eastern genomic group". This group included samples from populations such as [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans), [Bedouins](61770.md), [Jordanians](7515964.md), [Iraqi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Jew) and [Yemenite Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenite_Jews).[^cite_note-Kim_Choi_Kim_2023-123]&#x20;

**Haplogroups**&#x20;



Dendrogram based on mtDNA haplogroup frequencies from Badro et al. 2013.

A 2013 study by Badro et al. analyzed haplogroups of modern Palestinians as well as other groups from the Middle East. The study found that [mtDNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MtDNA) distribution of Palestinians, Lebanese, Jordanians, and Syrians clustered together separate from Yemenis, Saudis, and Egyptians, and that the Arabian peninsula population clusters were differentiated from Levantine populations.[^cite_note-138]&#x20;

In a [genetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_genealogy) study of [Y-chromosomal STRs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Y-chromosome_DNA_haplogroup) in two populations from Israel and the [Palestinian Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Territories), the Y-haplogroup frequency of Palestinian Christians was [E1b1b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_E-M123)(31.82%), followed by [G2a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_G-M201) (11.36%), and [J-M267](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_J-M267) (9.09%), and that of Palestinian Muslims was J-M267 (37.82%) followed by E1b1b (19.33%), and [T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_T-M184)(5.88%). The study sample consisted of 44 Palestinian Christians and 119 Palestinian Muslims.[^cite_note-139]&#x20;

A study found that the Palestinians, like Jordanians, Lebanese, and Syrians, have what appears to be female-mediated [gene flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_flow) in the form of [maternal DNA haplogroups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_mitochondrial_DNA_haplogroup) from [Sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md). 15 percent of the 117 Palestinian individuals tested carried such maternal haplogroups, consistent with female migration from eastern Africa into Near Eastern communities within the last few thousand years. It is likely this ancestry traces back to [women brought from Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Palestine) as part of the [Arab slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_slave_trade).[^cite_note-140]&#x20;

### Emergence of a distinct identity

&#x20;The timing and causes behind the emergence of a distinctively Palestinian national identity among the Arabs of Palestine are matters of scholarly disagreement. Some argue that it can be traced as far back as the [peasants' revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasants%27_revolt_in_Palestine) in 1834 (or even as early as the 17th century), while others argue that it did not emerge until after the Mandatory Palestine period.[^cite_note-Likhovski-41][^cite_note-Sorek-141] Legal historian Assaf Likhovski states that the prevailing view is that Palestinian identity originated in the early decades of the 20th century,[^cite_note-Likhovski-41] when an embryonic desire among Palestinians for self-government in the face of generalized fears that [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) would lead to a [Jewish state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_state) and the dispossession of the Arab majority crystallised among most editors, Christian and Muslim, of local newspapers.[^cite_note-142] The term itself *Filasṭīnī* was first introduced by [Khalīl Beidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalil_Beidas) in a translation of a Russian work on the Holy Land into Arabic in 1898. After that, its usage gradually spread so that, by 1908, with the loosening of censorship controls under late Ottoman rule, a number of Muslim, Christian and Jewish correspondents writing for newspapers began to use the term with great frequency in referring to the 'Palestinian people' (*ahl/ahālī Filasṭīn*), 'Palestinians' (*al-Filasṭīnīyūn*), the 'sons of Palestine' (*abnā' Filasṭīn*) or to 'Palestinian society' (*al-mujtama' al-filasṭīnī*).[^cite_note-ZachBeška-143]



[Eagle of Saladin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle_of_Saladin), the [coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Palestine) and emblem of the [Palestinian Authority](24093.md)

Whatever the differing viewpoints over the timing, causal mechanisms, and orientation of Palestinian nationalism, by the early 20th century strong opposition to Zionism and evidence of a burgeoning nationalistic Palestinian identity is found in the content of Arabic-language newspapers in Palestine, such as *[Al-Karmil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Karmil_\(newspaper\))* (est. 1908) and *[Filasteen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filastin_\(newspaper\))* (est. 1911).[^cite_note-Khalidip124-144] Filasteen initially focused its critique of Zionism around the failure of the Ottoman administration to control Jewish immigration and the large influx of foreigners, later exploring the impact of Zionist land-purchases on Palestinian peasants ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): فلاحين, *[fellahin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellahin)*), expressing growing concern over land dispossession and its implications for the society at large.[^cite_note-Khalidip124-144]&#x20;

Historian [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi)'s 1997 book *Palestinian Identity: The Construction of Modern National Consciousness* is considered a "foundational text" on the subject.[^cite_note-145] He notes that the archaeological strata that denote the history of [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) – encompassing the [Biblical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical), [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome), [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine), [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad), [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid), [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid), [Crusader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusade), [Ayyubid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid), [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate_\(Cairo\)) and [Ottoman](22278.md) periods – form part of the identity of the modern-day Palestinian people, as they have come to understand it over the last century.[^cite_note-Khalidip18-44] Noting that Palestinian identity has never been an exclusive one, with "Arabism, religion, and local loyalties" playing an important role, Khalidi cautions against the efforts of some extreme advocates of Palestinian nationalism to "anachronistically" read back into history a nationalist consciousness that is in fact "relatively modern".[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-Khalidip19-147]&#x20;

Khalidi argues that the modern national identity of Palestinians has its roots in [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) discourses that emerged among the peoples of the [Ottoman empire](22278.md) in the late 19th century that sharpened following the demarcation of modern nation-state boundaries in the [Middle East](19323.md) after [World War I](4764461.md).[^cite_note-Khalidip19-147] Khalidi also states that although the challenge posed by [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) played a role in shaping this identity, that "it is a serious mistake to suggest that Palestinian identity emerged mainly as a response to Zionism."[^cite_note-Khalidip19-147]&#x20;



1732 copy of the map by Ottoman geographer [Kâtip Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%A2tip_%C3%87elebi) (1609–57) from the first printed atlas in the Ottoman Empire, and represented the first detailed mapping of the Asian provinces of the empire.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETamari201727–29-148]

&#x20;with the term ارض فلسطين ("Land of Palestine") extending vertically down the length of the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River)

Conversely, historian [James L. Gelvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Gelvin) argues that [Palestinian nationalism](47246228.md) was a direct reaction to Zionism. In his book *The Israel-Palestine Conflict: One Hundred Years of War* he states that "Palestinian nationalism emerged during the interwar period in response to [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) immigration and settlement."[^cite_note-Gelvin_92-149] Gelvin argues that this fact does not make the Palestinian identity any less legitimate: "The fact that Palestinian nationalism developed later than Zionism and indeed in response to it does not in any way diminish the legitimacy of Palestinian nationalism or make it less valid than Zionism. All nationalisms arise in opposition to some 'other.' Why else would there be the need to specify who you are? And all nationalisms are defined by what they oppose."[^cite_note-Gelvin_92-149]&#x20;

David Seddon writes that "\[t]he creation of Palestinian identity in its contemporary sense was formed essentially during the 1960s, with the creation of the Palestine Liberation Organization." He adds, however, that "the existence of a population with a recognizably similar name ('the Philistines') in Biblical times suggests a degree of continuity over a long historical period (much as 'the Israelites' of the Bible suggest a long historical continuity in the same region)."[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

[Baruch Kimmerling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baruch_Kimmerling) and Joel S. Migdal consider the 1834 [Peasants' revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasants%27_revolt_in_Palestine) as constituting the first formative event of the Palestinian people. From 1516 to 1917, Palestine was ruled by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) save a decade from the 1830s to the 1840s when an Egyptian vassal of the Ottomans, [Muhammad Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt), and his son [Ibrahim Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Pasha_of_Egypt) successfully broke away from Ottoman leadership and, conquering territory spreading from Egypt to as far north as Damascus, asserted their own rule over the area. The so-called [Peasants' Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasants%27_Revolt_of_1834_\(Palestine\)) by Palestine's Arabs was precipitated by heavy demands for conscripts. The local leaders and urban notables were unhappy about the loss of traditional privileges, while the peasants were well aware that conscription was little more than a death sentence. Starting in May 1834 the rebels took many cities, among them [Jerusalem](16043.md), [Hebron](38577.md) and [Nablus](233369.md) and Ibrahim Pasha's army was deployed, defeating the last rebels on 4 August in Hebron.[^cite_note-Kimmerling6-151] [Benny Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benny_Morris) argues that the Arabs in Palestine nevertheless remained part of a larger national [pan-Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arab) or, alternatively, pan-Islamist movement.[^cite_note-152] [Walid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walid_Khalidi) argues otherwise, writing that Palestinians in [Ottoman](22278.md) times were "\[a]cutely aware of the distinctiveness of Palestinian history ..." and "\[a]lthough proud of their Arab heritage and ancestry, the Palestinians considered themselves to be descended not only from Arab conquerors of the seventh century but also from [indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples) who had lived in the country since time immemorial, including the ancient [Hebrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrews) and the [Canaanites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan#Canaanites) before them."[^cite_note-WKhalidi32-153]&#x20;



A 1930 Palestinian women's protest in [Jerusalem](16043.md) against the British Mandate. The sign reads "No dialogue, no negotiations until termination \[of the Mandate]"

[Bernard Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Lewis) argues it was not as a Palestinian nation that the Arabs of Ottoman Palestine objected to Zionists, since the very concept of such a nation was unknown to the Arabs of the area at the time and did not come into being until very much later. Even the concept of Arab nationalism in the Arab provinces of the Ottoman Empire, "had not reached significant proportions before the outbreak of World War I."[^cite_note-Lewis-43] Tamir Sorek, a [sociologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociologist), submits that, "Although a distinct Palestinian identity can be traced back at least to the middle of the nineteenth century (Kimmerling and Migdal 1993; Khalidi 1997b), or even to the seventeenth century (Gerber 1998), it was not until after World War I that a broad range of optional *political* affiliations became relevant for the Arabs of Palestine."[^cite_note-Sorek-141]&#x20;

Israeli historian [Efraim Karsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efraim_Karsh) takes the view that the Palestinian identity did not develop until after the [1967 war](29328.md) because the Palestinian exodus/expulsion had fractured society so greatly that it was impossible to piece together a national identity. Between 1948 and 1967, the Jordanians and other Arab countries hosting Arab refugees from Palestine/Israel silenced any expression of Palestinian identity and occupied their lands until Israel's conquests of 1967. The formal annexation of the West Bank by Jordan in 1950, and the subsequent granting of its Palestinian residents Jordanian citizenship, further stunted the growth of a Palestinian national identity by integrating them into Jordanian society.[^cite_note-154]&#x20;

The idea of a unique Palestinian state distinct from its Arab neighbors was at first rejected by Palestinian representatives. The [First Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Arab_Congress) of [Muslim-Christian Associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim-Christian_Associations) (in [Jerusalem](16043.md), February 1919), which met for the purpose of selecting a Palestinian Arab representative for the [Paris Peace Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Conference,_1919), adopted the following resolution: "We consider Palestine as part of Arab [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)), as it has never been separated from it at any time. We are connected with it by national, religious, [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language), natural, economic and geographical bonds."[^cite_note-155]&#x20;

## Rise of Palestinian nationalism

See also: [Palestinian nationalism](47246228.md)



1981 UN stamp to commemorate the Palestinian struggle

An independent Palestinian state has not exercised full [sovereignty](28791.md) over the land in which the Palestinians have lived during the modern era. Palestine was administered by the Ottoman Empire until World War I, and then overseen by the British Mandatory authorities. Israel was established in parts of Palestine in 1948, and in the wake of the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), [the West Bank was ruled by Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordanian_annexation_of_the_West_Bank), and the [Gaza Strip by Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Gaza_Strip_by_Egypt), with both countries continuing to administer these areas until [Israel occupied](2781576.md) them in the [Six-Day War](29328.md). Historian [Avi Shlaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avi_Shlaim) states that the Palestinians' lack of sovereignty over the land has been used by Israelis to deny Palestinians their rights to self-determination.[^cite_note-Attapatu-156]&#x20;

Today, the right of the Palestinian people to [self-determination](29269.md) has been affirmed by the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md), the [International Court of Justice](14918.md)[^cite_note-ICJ-157] and several Israeli authorities.[^cite_note-158] A total of 158 states [recognize Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_Palestine) as a state.[^cite_note-159] However, Palestinian sovereignty over the areas claimed as part of the Palestinian state remains limited, and the boundaries of the state remain a point of contestation between Palestinians and Israelis.&#x20;

### British Mandate (1917–1947)

Main article: [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine)



Mandatory Palestine in 1946

The first Palestinian nationalist organizations emerged at the end of the [World War I](4764461.md).[^cite_note-160] Two political factions emerged. *[al-Muntada al-Adabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Muntada_al-Adabi)*, dominated by the [Nashashibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashashibi) family, militated for the promotion of the Arabic language and culture, for the defense of Islamic values and for an independent Syria and Palestine. In [Damascus](8914.md), *al-Nadi al-Arabi*, dominated by the [Husayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husayni) family, defended the same values.[^cite_note-161]&#x20;

&#x20;Article 22 of The Covenant of the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) conferred an international legal status upon the territories and people which had ceased to be under the sovereignty of the Ottoman Empire as part of a 'sacred trust of civilization'. Article 7 of the League of Nations Mandate required the establishment of a new, separate, Palestinian nationality for the inhabitants. This meant that Palestinians did not become British citizens, and that Palestine was not annexed into the British dominions.[^cite_note-162] The Mandate document divided the population into Jewish and non-Jewish, and Britain, the Mandatory Power considered the Palestinian population to be composed of religious, not national, groups. Consequently, government censuses in 1922 and 1931 would categorize Palestinians confessionally as Muslims, Christians and Jews, with the category of Arab absent.[^cite_note-163]



[Musa Alami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_Alami) (1897–1984) was a Palestinian nationalist and politician, viewed in the 1940s as a leader of the Palestinians

The articles of the Mandate mentioned the civil and religious rights of the non-Jewish communities in Palestine, but not their political status. At the [San Remo conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Remo_conference), it was decided to accept the text of those articles, while inserting in the minutes of the conference an undertaking by the Mandatory Power that this would not involve the surrender of any of the rights hitherto enjoyed by the non-Jewish communities in Palestine. In 1922, the British authorities over Mandatory Palestine proposed a draft constitution that would have granted the Palestinian Arabs representation in a Legislative Council on condition that they accept the terms of the mandate. The Palestine Arab delegation rejected the proposal as "wholly unsatisfactory", noting that "the People of Palestine" could not accept the inclusion of the Balfour Declaration in the constitution's preamble as the basis for discussions. They further took issue with the designation of Palestine as a British "colony of the lowest order."[^cite_note-164] The Arabs tried to get the British to offer an Arab legal establishment again roughly ten years later, but to no avail.[^cite_note-Continuum-165]&#x20;

After the British general, Louis Bols, read out the [Balfour Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration) in February 1920, some 1,500 Palestinians demonstrated in the streets of Jerusalem.[^cite_note-HistoryOfPalestinianRevolts-166]&#x20;

A month later, during the 1920 Nebi Musa riots, the protests against British rule and Jewish immigration became violent and Bols banned all demonstrations. In May 1921 however, further anti-Jewish riots [broke out in Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_riots_\(May_1921\)) and dozens of Arabs and Jews were killed in the confrontations.[^cite_note-HistoryOfPalestinianRevolts-166]&#x20;

After the [1920 Nebi Musa riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_Nebi_Musa_riots), the [San Remo conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Remo_conference) and the failure of [Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq) to establish the Kingdom of [Greater Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Syria), a distinctive form of Palestinian Arab nationalism took root between April and July 1920.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] With the fall of the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) and the French conquest of [Syria](7515849.md), coupled with the British conquest and administration of Palestine, the formerly pan-Syrianist [mayor of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Jerusalem), [Musa Qasim Pasha al-Husayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_Qasim_Pasha_al-Husayni), said "Now, after the recent events in [Damascus](8914.md), we have to effect a complete change in our plans here. Southern Syria no longer exists. We must defend Palestine".[^cite_note-169]&#x20;

Conflict between Palestinian nationalists and various types of pan-Arabists continued during the British Mandate, but the latter became increasingly marginalized. Two prominent leaders of the Palestinian nationalists were [Mohammad Amin al-Husayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Amin_al-Husayni), Grand Mufti of Jerusalem, appointed by the British, and [Izz ad-Din al-Qassam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_ad-Din_al-Qassam).[^cite_note-HistoryOfPalestinianRevolts-166] After the killing of sheikh Izz ad-Din al-Qassam by the British in 1935, his followers initiated the [1936–39 Arab revolt in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%9339_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine), which began with a [general strike](41138909.md) in Jaffa and attacks on Jewish and British installations in [Nablus](233369.md).[^cite_note-HistoryOfPalestinianRevolts-166] The [Arab Higher Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Higher_Committee) called for a nationwide general strike, non-payment of taxes, and the closure of municipal governments, and demanded an end to Jewish immigration and a ban of the sale of land to Jews. By the end of 1936, the movement had become a national revolt, and resistance grew during 1937 and 1938. In response, the British declared [martial law](50477.md), dissolved the Arab High Committee and arrested officials from the Supreme Muslim Council who were behind the revolt. By 1939, 5,000 Arabs had been killed in British attempts to quash the revolt; more than 15,000 were wounded.[^cite_note-HistoryOfPalestinianRevolts-166]&#x20;

### War (1947–1949)

Main article: [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war)



[Abd al-Qadir al-Husayni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Qadir_al-Husayni), leader of the [Army of the Holy War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_the_Holy_War) in 1948

In November 1947, the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) adopted the [Partition Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Partition_Plan_for_Palestine), which divided the mandate of Palestine into two states: one majority Arab and one majority Jewish. The Palestinian Arabs rejected the plan and attacked Jewish civilian areas and paramilitary targets. Following [Israel's declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence) in May 1948, five Arab armies (Lebanon, Egypt, Syria, Iraq, and Transjordan) came to the Palestinian Arabs' aid against the newly founded [State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel).[^cite_note-Milestones-170]&#x20;

The Palestinian Arabs suffered such a major defeat at the end of the war, that the term they use to describe the war is [Nakba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakba) (the "catastrophe").[^cite_note-Caplan-171] Israel took control of much of the territory that would have been allocated to the Arab state had the Palestinian Arabs accepted the UN partition plan.[^cite_note-Milestones-170] Along with a military defeat, hundreds of thousands of Palestinians [fled or were expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_exodus) from what became the State of Israel. Israel did not allow the [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) of the war to return to Israel.[^cite_note-172]&#x20;

### "Lost years" (1949–1967)

---

&#x20;   [Gaza](12047.md) and the [West Bank](33209.md) (including [East Jerusalem](343508.md)) under Egyptian and Jordanian control till [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6_Day_War)

After the war, there was a hiatus in Palestinian political activity. Khalidi attributes this to the traumatic events of 1947–49, which included the depopulation of over [400 towns and villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depopulated_Palestinian_locations_in_Israel) and the creation of hundreds of thousands of refugees.[^cite_note-Khalidi178-173] 418 villages had been razed, 46,367 buildings, 123 schools, 1,233 mosques, 8 churches and 68 holy shrines, many with a long history, destroyed by Israeli forces.[^cite_note-174] In addition, Palestinians lost from 1.5 to 2 million acres of land, an estimated 150,000 urban and rural homes, and 23,000 commercial structures such as shops and offices.[^cite_note-175] Recent estimates of the cost to Palestinians in property confiscations by Israel from 1948 onwards has concluded that under international law the fair value of compensation to Palestinians would total \$300 billion in 2008–2009 prices.[^cite_note-Anderson-176]&#x20;

Those parts of British Mandatory Palestine which did not become part of the newly declared Israeli state were occupied by Egypt or annexed by Jordan. At the [Jericho Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jericho_Conference) on 1 December 1948, 2,000 Palestinian delegates supported a resolution calling for "the unification of Palestine and Transjordan as a step toward full Arab unity".[^cite_note-177] During what Khalidi terms the "lost years" that followed, Palestinians lacked a center of gravity, divided as they were between these countries and others such as Syria, Lebanon, and elsewhere.[^cite_note-Khalidi179-178]&#x20;

In the 1950s, a new generation of Palestinian nationalist groups and movements began to organize clandestinely, stepping out onto the public stage in the 1960s.[^cite_note-Khalidi180-179] The traditional Palestinian elite who had dominated negotiations with the British and the Zionists in the Mandate, and who were largely held responsible for the loss of Palestine, were replaced by these new movements whose recruits generally came from poor to middle-class backgrounds and were often students or recent graduates of universities in [Cairo](6293.md), [Beirut](37428.md) and Damascus.[^cite_note-Khalidi180-179] The potency of the [pan-Arabist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism) ideology put forward by [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser)—popular among Palestinians for whom Arabism was already an important component of their identity[^cite_note-Khalidi182-180]—tended to obscure the identities of the separate Arab states it subsumed.[^cite_note-Khalidi181-181]&#x20;

### 1967–present

See also: [Six-Day War](29328.md)

&#x20;Since 1967, Palestinians in the West Bank and the Gaza Strip have lived under military occupation, creating, according to Avram Bornstein, a [carceralization of their society](19008450.md).[^cite_note-182] In the meantime, pan-Arabism has waned as an aspect of Palestinian identity. The Israeli occupation of the Gaza Strip and West Bank triggered a [second Palestinian exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Palestinian_exodus) and fractured Palestinian political and militant groups, prompting them to give up residual hopes in pan-Arabism. They rallied increasingly around the [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO), which had been formed in Cairo in 1964. The group grew in popularity in the following years, especially under the nationalistic orientation of the leadership of [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat).[^cite_note-plo1974-183] Mainstream [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) Palestinian nationalism was grouped together under the umbrella of the PLO whose constituent organizations include [Fatah](11104.md) and the [Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine), among other groups who at that time believed that [political violence](2646043.md) was the only way to "liberate" Palestine.[^cite_note-Khalidip18-44] These groups gave voice to a tradition that emerged in the 1960s that argues Palestinian nationalism has deep historical roots, with extreme advocates reading a Palestinian nationalist consciousness and identity back into the history of Palestine over the past few centuries, and even millennia, when such a consciousness is in fact relatively modern.[^cite_note-Khalidip.149n-184]



[Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat), [Nayef Hawatmeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nayef_Hawatmeh) and [Kamal Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamal_Nasser) in a Jordan press conference in Amman, 1970

The [Battle of Karameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Karameh) and the events of [Black September in Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_September_in_Jordan) contributed to growing Palestinian support for these groups, particularly among Palestinians in exile. Concurrently, among Palestinians in the West Bank and Gaza Strip, a new ideological theme, known as *[sumud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumud)*, represented the Palestinian political strategy popularly adopted from 1967 onward. As a concept closely related to the land, agriculture and [indigenousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_\(people\)), the ideal image of the Palestinian put forward at this time was that of the peasant (in Arabic, *[fellah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellah)*) who stayed put on his land, refusing to leave. A strategy more passive than that adopted by the [Palestinian fedayeen](14068131.md), *sumud* provided an important subtext to the narrative of the fighters, "in symbolizing continuity and connections with the land, with peasantry and a rural way of life."[^cite_note-Schulzp105-185]&#x20;

In 1974, the PLO was recognized as the sole legitimate representative of the Palestinian people by the Arab nation-states and was granted observer status as a national [liberation movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_movement) by the United Nations that same year.[^cite_note-IMEU-46][^cite_note-186] Israel rejected the resolution, calling it "shameful".[^cite_note-Allon-187] In a speech to the [Knesset](74656.md), Deputy Premier and Foreign Minister [Yigal Allon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yigal_Allon) outlined the government's view that: "No one can expect us to recognize the terrorist organization called the PLO as representing the Palestinians—because it does not. No one can expect us to negotiate with the heads of terror-gangs, who through their ideology and actions, endeavor to liquidate the State of Israel."[^cite_note-Allon-187]&#x20;

In 1975, the United Nations established a subsidiary organ, the [Committee on the Exercise of the Inalienable Rights of the Palestinian People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Exercise_of_the_Inalienable_Rights_of_the_Palestinian_People), to recommend a program of implementation to enable the Palestinian people to exercise national independence and their rights to self-determination without external interference, national independence and sovereignty, and to return to their homes and property.[^cite_note-188]&#x20;



Protest for Palestine in [Tunisia](30188.md)

The [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) (1987–93) was the first popular uprising against the Israeli occupation of 1967. Followed by the PLO's 1988 proclamation of a [State of Palestine](241405.md), these developments served to further reinforce the Palestinian national identity. After the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1991, Kuwaiti authorities forcibly pressured nearly 200,000 Palestinians to [leave Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_exodus_from_Kuwait_\(Gulf_War\)).[^cite_note-ppp-189] The policy which partly led to this exodus was a response to the alignment of PLO leader Yasser Arafat with [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein).&#x20;

The [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords), the first Israeli–Palestinian interim peace agreement, were signed in 1993. The process was envisioned to last five years, ending in June 1999, when the withdrawal of Israeli forces from the Gaza Strip and the Jericho area began. The expiration of this term without the recognition by Israel of the Palestinian State and without the effective termination of the occupation was followed by the [Second Intifada](71717.md) in 2000.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] The second intifada was more violent than the first.[^cite_note-192] The International Court of Justice observed that since the government of Israel had decided to recognize the PLO as the representative of the Palestinian people, their existence was no longer an issue. The court noted that the Israeli-Palestinian Interim Agreement on the West Bank and the Gaza Strip of 28 September 1995 also referred a number of times to the Palestinian people and its "legitimate rights".[^cite_note-193] According to [Thomas Giegerich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Giegerich), with respect to the Palestinian people's right to form a sovereign independent state, "The right of self-determination gives the Palestinian people collectively the inalienable right freely to determine its political status, while Israel, having recognized the Palestinians as a separate people, is obliged to promote and respect this right in conformity with the Charter of the United Nations".[^cite_note-Giegerich-194]&#x20;

Following the failures of the Second Intifada, a younger generation is emerging that cares less about nationalist ideology than about economic growth. This has been a source of tension between some of the Palestinian political leadership and Palestinian business professionals who desire economic cooperation with Israelis. At an international conference in Bahrain, Palestinian businessman Ashraf Jabari said, "I have no problem working with Israel. It is time to move on. ... The Palestinian Authority does not want peace. They told the families of the businessmen that they are wanted \[by police] for participating in the Bahrain workshop."[^cite_note-195]&#x20;

Since the late 2000s, Palestinians experienced persistent political fragmentation between the [Fatah](11104.md)-led [Palestinian Authority](24093.md) in the West Bank and the [Hamas](13913.md) administration in Gaza. Efforts by Prime Minister [Salam Fayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salam_Fayyad) to professionalize governance in the West Bank achieved administrative improvements but were criticized for lacking a political foundation. By the mid-2010s, Palestinian institutions weakened further as reconciliation efforts faltered and external pressures increased. Hamas consolidated control in Gaza, while Palestinian Authority structures in the West Bank lost credibility amid ineffectual diplomacy.&#x20;

The [October 7, 2023 Hamas attacks on Israel](74998317.md) and the subsequent war in Gaza deepened this crisis, devastating Gaza's infrastructure and reshaping regional and international discourse.[^cite_note-196] As Palestinians are "a national group," of which the Palestinians of the Gaza Strip form a part, the [International Committee of Jurists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Committee_of_Jurists), among other legal experts, have called on States to prevent [genocide in Gaza](75668427.md).[^cite_note-197] South Africa has initiated a case against Israel at the [International Court of Justice](14918.md), accusing Israel of committing genocide. The case is ongoing.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

Despite the conflict, Israelis and Palestinians inhabit an interlinked ecosystem in which cooperation remains vital for public health and environmental protection, with potential for mutual benefits.[^cite_note-199]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of the Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Palestinian_territories), [Demographics of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Israel), and [Demographics of Jordan](15717.md)

In the absence of a comprehensive census including all Palestinian diaspora populations, and those that have remained within what was [British Mandate Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), exact population figures are difficult to determine. The [Palestinian Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) (PCBS) announced at the end of 2015 that the number of Palestinians worldwide at the end of 2015 was 12.37 million of which the number still residing within historic Palestine was 6.22 million.[^cite_note-Maan1116-203] In 2022, [Arnon Soffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Soffer) estimated that in the territory of former Mandatory Palestine (now encompassing Israel and the Palestinian territories of the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md)), there's a Palestinian population of 7.503 million, making up 51.16% of the total population.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205] Within Israel proper, Palestinians constitute almost 21 percent of the population as part of its [Arab citizens](492331.md).[^cite_note-critical-49]&#x20;

In 2005, a critical review of the PCBS figures and methodology was conducted by the American-Israel Demographic Research Group (AIDRG).[^cite_note-206] In their report,[^cite_note-207] they claimed that several errors in the PCBS methodology and assumptions artificially inflated the numbers by a total of 1.3 million. The PCBS numbers were cross-checked against a variety of other sources (e.g., asserted [birth rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_rates) based on [fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility) rate assumptions for a given year were checked against Palestinian Ministry of Health figures as well as Ministry of Education school enrollment figures six years later; immigration numbers were checked against numbers collected at border crossings, etc.). The errors claimed in their analysis included: birth rate errors (308,000), immigration & emigration errors (310,000), failure to account for migration to Israel (105,000), double-counting [Jerusalem](16043.md) Arabs (210,000), counting former residents now living abroad (325,000) and other discrepancies (82,000). The results of their research was also presented before the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md) on 8 March 2006.[^cite_note-208]&#x20;

The study was criticised by [Sergio DellaPergola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_DellaPergola), a demographer at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem.[^cite_note-Azure-209] DellaPergola accused the authors of the AIDRG report of misunderstanding basic principles of demography on account of their lack of expertise in the subject, but he also acknowledged that he did not take into account the emigration of Palestinians and thinks it has to be examined, as well as the birth and mortality statistics of the Palestinian Authority.[^cite_note-210] He also accused AIDRG of selective use of data and multiple systematic errors in their analysis, claiming that the authors assumed the Palestinian Electoral registry to be complete even though registration is voluntary, and they used an unrealistically low Total Fertility Ratio (a statistical abstraction of births per woman) to reanalyse that data in a "typical circular mistake." DellaPergola estimated the Palestinian population of the West Bank and Gaza at the end of 2005 as 3.33 million, or 3.57 million if East Jerusalem is included. These figures are only slightly lower than the official Palestinian figures.[^cite_note-Azure-209] The [Israeli Civil Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Civil_Administration) put the number of Palestinians in the West Bank at 2,657,029 as of May 2012.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]&#x20;

The AIDRG study was also criticized by [Ian Lustick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Lustick), who accused its authors of multiple methodological errors and a political agenda.[^cite_note-213]&#x20;

In 2009, at the request of the PLO, "Jordan revoked the citizenship of thousands of Palestinians to keep them from remaining permanently in the country."[^cite_note-214]&#x20;

Many Palestinians have settled in the United States, particularly in the Chicago area.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]&#x20;

In total, an estimated 600,000 Palestinians are thought to reside in the Americas. Palestinian [emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) to [South America](26769.md) began for economic reasons that pre-dated the Arab-Israeli conflict, but continued to grow thereafter.[^cite_note-217] Many emigrants were from the [Bethlehem](4312.md) area. Those emigrating to Latin America were mainly Christian. Half of those of Palestinian origin in [Latin America](18524.md) live in [Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinians_in_Chile),[^cite_note-Diario_La_Nación,_2009-10] [El Salvador](9356.md)[^cite_note-218] and [Honduras](13394.md)[^cite_note-219] also have substantial Palestinian populations. These two countries have had presidents of Palestinian [ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestry) ([Antonio Saca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Saca) in El Salvador and [Carlos Roberto Flores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Roberto_Flores) in Honduras). [Belize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belize), which has a smaller Palestinian population, has a Palestinian [minister](739237.md) – [Said Musa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Musa).[^cite_note-220] [Schafik Jorge Handal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schafik_Jorge_Handal), Salvadoran politician and former [guerrilla](12720.md) leader, was the son of Palestinian immigrants.[^cite_note-Mendez-221]&#x20;
