[ ](23267.md)

Clickable map of the [more than 400 depopulated towns and villages of the 1948 Palestinian exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depopulated_Palestinian_locations_in_Israel) (red) and the c.60 modern day [Palestinian refugee camps](400347.md) (blue)&#x20;

&#x20;In 2006, there were 4,255,120 Palestinians registered as [refugees](74681.md) with the [United Nations Relief and Works Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Relief_and_Works_Agency) (UNRWA). This number includes the [descendants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinship) of refugees who fled or were expelled during the 1948 war, but excludes those who have since then emigrated to areas outside of UNRWA's remit.[^cite_note-UNRWA-222] Based on these figures, almost half of all Palestinians are registered refugees. The 993,818 Palestinian refugees in the Gaza Strip and 705,207 Palestinian refugees in the West Bank, who hail from towns and villages now located within the borders of Israel, are included in these figures.[^cite_note-223]



Palestinian refugees in 1948

UNRWA figures do not include some 274,000 people, or 1 in 5.5 of all Arab residents of Israel, who are [internally displaced Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_Displaced_Palestinians) refugees.[^cite_note-Badil-224][^cite_note-IDMC-225] According to British historian [Perry Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_Anderson), it is estimated that half of the population in the Palestinian territories are refugees.[^cite_note-Anderson-176]&#x20;

Palestinian refugee camps in Lebanon, Syria, Jordan, and the West Bank are organized according to a refugee family's village or place of origin. Among the first things that children born in the camps learn is the name of their village of origin. David McDowall writes that, "\[...] a yearning for Palestine permeates the whole refugee community and is most ardently espoused by the younger refugees, for whom home exists only in the imagination."[^cite_note-McDowall90-226]&#x20;

Israeli policy to prevent the refugees from returning to their homes was initially formulated by David Ben Gurion and [Joseph Weitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Weitz), director of the [Jewish National Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_National_Fund) was formally adopted by the Israeli cabinet in June 1948.[^cite_note-227] In December of that year the UN adopted [resolution 194](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolution_194), which resolved "that the refugees wishing to return to their homes and live at peace with their neighbors should be permitted to do so at the earliest practicable date, and that compensation should be paid for the property of those choosing not to return and for loss of or damage to property which, under principles of international law or in equity, should be made good by the Governments or authorities responsible."[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-MG-229][^cite_note-MM-230] Despite much of the international community, including the US President Harry Truman, insisting that the repatriation of Palestinian refugees was essential, Israel refused to accept the principle.[^cite_note-MM-230] In the intervening years Israel has consistently refused to change its position and has introduced further legislation to hinder Palestinians refugees from returning and reclaiming their land and confiscated property.[^cite_note-MG-229][^cite_note-MM-230]&#x20;

In keeping with an Arab League resolution in 1965, most Arab countries have refused to grant citizenship to Palestinians, arguing that it would be a threat to their [right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return) to their homes in Palestine.[^cite_note-MG-229][^cite_note-Jpost287479-231] In 2012, Egypt deviated from this practice by granting citizenship to 50,000 Palestinians, mostly from the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-Jpost287479-231]&#x20;

Palestinians living in Lebanon are deprived of basic civil rights. They cannot own homes or land and are barred from becoming lawyers, engineers and doctors.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;

### Religion

See also: [Religion in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Palestine), [Palestinian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Muslims), and [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians)



Praying Palestinians in Gaza in 2009.

&#x20;The majority of Palestinians are Muslim,[^cite_note-233] the vast majority of whom are followers of the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) branch of [Islam](6037917.md),[^cite_note-234] with a small minority of [Ahmadiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya).[^cite_note-235] [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) represent a significant minority of 6%, and belong to several denominations, followed by much smaller religious communities, including [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans). [Palestinian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Jews) – considered Palestinian by the [Palestinian National Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Charter) adopted by the Palestine Liberation Organization (PLO) which defined them as those "Jews who had normally resided in Palestine until the beginning of the [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) invasion" – today identify as Israelis[^cite_note-236] (with the exception of a very few individuals). Palestinian Jews almost universally abandoned any such identity after the establishment of Israel and their incorporation into the [Israeli Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Israel) population, which was originally composed of [Jewish immigrants](351513.md) from around the world.



Silhouette of [East Jerusalem](343508.md)

Until the end of the 19th century, cross-cultural [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) between Islamic and Christian symbols and figures in religious practice was common in the Palestinian countryside, where most villages did not have local mosques or churches.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237] Popular feast days, such as [Thursday of the Dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thursday_of_the_Dead), were celebrated by both Muslims and Christians and shared prophets and saints include [Jonah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonah), who is venerated in [Halhul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halhul) as both a Biblical and Islamic prophet, and [St. George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George), who is known in Arabic as [al-Khdir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khidr). Villagers would pay tribute to local patron saints at [maqams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqam_\(shrine\)) – domed single rooms often placed in the shadow of an ancient [carob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carob) or [oak tree](39696.md); many of them are rooted in Jewish, Samaritan, Christian and sometimes pagan traditions.[^cite_note-238] Saints, taboo by the standards of orthodox Islam, mediated between man and God, and shrines to saints and holy men dotted the Palestinian landscape.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237] Ali Qleibo, a Palestinian [anthropologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropologist), states that this built evidence constitutes "an architectural testimony to Christian/Moslem Palestinian religious sensibility and its roots in [ancient Semitic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Semitic_religion)."[^cite_note-Qleibo-237]&#x20;

Religion as constitutive of individual identity was accorded a minor role within Palestinian social structure until the latter half of the 19th century.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237] Jean Moretain, a priest writing in 1848, wrote that a Christian in Palestine was "distinguished only by the fact that he belonged to a particular clan. If a certain tribe was Christian, then an individual would be Christian, but without knowledge of what distinguished his faith from that of a Muslim."[^cite_note-Qleibo-237]&#x20;



Christians from [Gaza](12047.md)

The concessions granted to [France](5843419.md) and other Western powers by the Ottoman Sultanate in the aftermath of the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) had a significant impact on contemporary Palestinian religious cultural identity.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237] Religion was transformed into an element "constituting the individual/collective identity in conformity with orthodox precepts", and formed a major building block in the political development of Palestinian nationalism.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237]&#x20;

The [British census of 1922](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1922_census_of_Palestine) registered 752,048 inhabitants in Palestine, consisting of 660,641 Palestinian Arabs (Muslim and Christian Arabs), 83,790 Palestinian Jews, and 7,617 persons belonging to other groups. The corresponding percentage breakdown is 87% Muslim and Christian Arab and 11% Jewish.[^cite_note-239]&#x20;



Palestinian [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) family making bread 1920

Bernard Sabella of [Bethlehem University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethlehem_University) estimates that 6% of the Palestinian population worldwide is Christian and that 56% of them live outside of historic Palestine.[^cite_note-240] According to the [Palestinian Academic Society for the Study of International Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Academic_Society_for_the_Study_of_International_Affairs), the Palestinian population of the West Bank and Gaza Strip is 97% Muslim and 3% Christian. The vast majority of the [Palestinian community in Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_community_in_Chile) follow Christianity, largely [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) and some [Roman Catholic](606848.md), and in fact the number of Palestinian Christians in [the diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_diaspora) in Chile alone exceeds the number of those who have remained in their homeland.[^cite_note-Holston-241] [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George) is the patron saint of the Palestinian Christians.[^cite_note-242]&#x20;

The [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) became Israeli citizens and Druze males serve in the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md), though some individuals identify as "Palestinian Druze".[^cite_note-243] According to Salih al-Shaykh, most Druze do not consider themselves to be Palestinian: "their Arab identity emanates in the main from the common language and their socio-cultural background, but is detached from any national political conception. It is not directed at Arab countries or Arab nationality or the Palestinian people, and does not express sharing any fate with them. From this point of view, their identity is Israel, and this identity is stronger than their Arab identity".[^cite_note-244]&#x20;

There are also about 350 [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) who carry Palestinian identity cards and live in the West Bank while a roughly equal number live in [Holon](231871.md) and carry Israeli citizenship.[^cite_note-Amid-245] Those who live in the West Bank also are represented in the legislature for the Palestinian National Authority.[^cite_note-Amid-245] They are commonly referred to among Palestinians as the "Jews of Palestine", and maintain their own unique cultural identity.[^cite_note-Amid-245]&#x20;

Jews who identify as Palestinian Jews are few, but include Israeli Jews who are part of the [Neturei Karta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neturei_Karta) group,[^cite_note-246] and [Uri Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uri_Davis), an Israeli citizen and self-described Palestinian Jew (who converted to Islam in 2008 in order to marry Miyassar Abu Ali) who serves as an observer member in the [Palestine National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_National_Council).[^cite_note-247]&#x20;

[Bahá'u'lláh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%27u%27ll%C3%A1h), founder of the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) spent his last years in [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel), then part of the Ottoman Empire. He remained there for 24 years, where a [shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_Bah%C3%A1%27u%27ll%C3%A1h) was erected in his honor.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-PSmith26-249]&#x20;

### Current demographics

According to the PCBS, there are an estimated 4,816,503 Palestinians in the Palestinian territories as of 2016, of whom 2,935,368 live in the West Bank and 1,881,135 in the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-PCBS-250] According to the [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics), there were 1,658,000 Arab citizens of Israel as of 2013.[^cite_note-ICBS2013-251] Both figures include Palestinians in East Jerusalem.&#x20;

In 2008, Minority Rights Group International estimated the number of Palestinians in Jordan to be about 3 million.[^cite_note-MRPal-252] The [UNRWA](32270.md) put their number at 2.3 million as of 2024.[^cite_note-unjo-6]&#x20;

## Society

### Language

See also: [Languages of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Palestine) and [Palestinian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabic)



[Areen Omari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Areen_Omari), a Palestinian actress and producer, attends a motion picture ceremony

Palestinian Arabic is a subgroup of the broader [Levantine Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic) dialect. Prior to the 7th century Islamic Conquest and [Arabization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabization) of the Levant, the primary languages spoken in Palestine, among the predominantly [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) and [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Jews) communities, were [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), and [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language).[^cite_note-253] [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) was also spoken in some areas.[^cite_note-254] Palestinian Arabic, like other variations of the [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) dialect, exhibits substantial influences in [lexicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicon) from Aramaic.[^cite_note-Greenfieldp158-255]&#x20;

Palestinian Arabic has three primary sub-variations, Rural, Urban, and Bedouin, with the pronunciation of the *Qāf* serving as a [shibboleth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shibboleth) to distinguish between the three main Palestinian sub-dialects: The urban variety notes a \[Q] sound, while the rural variety (spoken in the villages around major cities) have a \[K] for the \[Q]. The Bedouin variety of Palestine (spoken mainly in the southern region and along the Jordan valley) use a \[G] instead of \[Q].[^cite_note-Ammon-256]&#x20;

It is well established in scholarship that Palestinians, particularly the [fellahin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellah) have preserved the original [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_root) [place names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_names_in_Palestine) of many sites mentioned in the Bible, as was documented by the American geographer [Edward Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Robinson_\(scholar\)) in the 19th century.[^cite_note-Parmenter11-257]&#x20;

Palestinians who live or work in Israel generally can also speak [Modern Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew), as do some who live in the West Bank and Gaza Strip.&#x20;

### Education

Main articles: [Education in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Palestine) and [Education in Israel § Arab sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Israel#Arab_sector)



Palestinian schoolgirls



Palestinian students and [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry)

The literacy rate of Palestine was 96.3% according to a 2014 report by the [United Nations Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme), which is high by international standards. There is a gender difference in the population aged above 15 with 5.9% of women considered illiterate compared to 1.6% of men.[^cite_note-UNDP2014-258] Illiteracy among women has fallen from 20.3% in 1997 to less than 6% in 2014.[^cite_note-UNDP2014-258]&#x20;

Palestinian intellectuals, among them [May Ziadeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Ziadeh) and [Khalil Beidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalil_Beidas), were an integral part of the Arab intelligentsia. Educational levels among Palestinians have traditionally been high. In the 1960s the West Bank had a higher percentage of its adolescent population enrolled in high school education than did Lebanon.[^cite_note-259] [Claude Cheysson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claude_Cheysson), France's Minister for Foreign Affairs under the first [Mitterrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mitterrand) Presidency, held in the mid-eighties that, 'even thirty years ago, (Palestinians) probably already had the largest educated elite of all the Arab peoples.'[^cite_note-260]&#x20;

Contributions to Palestinian culture have been made by diaspora figures including [Edward Said](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Said) and [Ghada Karmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghada_Karmi), Arab citizens of Israel including [Emile Habibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emile_Habibi), and Jordanians including [Ibrahim Nasrallah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Nasrallah).[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-Pontas-262]&#x20;

### Women and family

Main article: [Women in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Palestine)

In the 19th and early 20th century, there were some well known Palestinian families, which included the [Khalidi family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalidi_family), the [al-Husayni family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Husayni_family), the [Nashashibi family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashashibi_family), the [Tuqan family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuqan_family), the [Nusaybah family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusaybah_family), [Qudwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qudwa) family, [Shawish clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawish_clan), [Shurrab family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shurrab_family), Al-Zaghab family, [Al-Khalil family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Khalil_family), [Ridwan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridwan_dynasty), Al-Zeitawi family, [Abu Ghosh clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghosh_clan), [Barghouti family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barghouti_family), [Doghmush clan](12081036.md), [Douaihy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douaihy) family, [Hilles clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilles_clan), [Jarrar family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarrar_family), and the [Jayyusi family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayyusi_family). Since various conflicts with Zionists began, some of the communities have subsequently left Palestine. The role of women varies among Palestinians, with both progressive and ultra-conservative opinions existing. Other groups of Palestinians, such as the [Negev Bedouins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Bedouin) or [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), may no longer self-identify as Palestinian for political reasons.[^cite_note-263]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Palestine)

&#x20;Ali Qleibo, a Palestinian [anthropologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropologist), has critiqued Muslim historiography for assigning the beginning of Palestinian cultural identity to the advent of Islam in the 7th century. In describing the effect of such historiography, he writes:&#x20;

> [Pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism) origins are disavowed. As such the peoples who populated Palestine throughout history have discursively rescinded their own history and religion as they adopted the religion, language, and culture of Islam.[^cite_note-Qleibo-237]

&#x20;That the peasant culture of the large [fellahin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellahin) class showed features of cultures other than Islam was a conclusion arrived at by some Western scholars and explorers who mapped and surveyed Palestine during the latter half of the 19th century,[^cite_note-264] and these ideas were to influence 20th-century debates on Palestinian identity by local and international ethnographers. The contributions of the 'nativist' [ethnographies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnography) produced by [Tawfiq Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfiq_Canaan) and other Palestinian writers and published in *The Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* (1920–48) were driven by the concern that the "native culture of Palestine", and in particular peasant society, was being undermined by the forces of [modernity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernity).[^cite_note-Tamari-265] Salim Tamari writes that:&#x20;

> Implicit in their scholarship (and made explicit by Canaan himself) was another theme, namely that the peasants of Palestine represent—through their folk norms ... the living heritage of all the accumulated ancient cultures that had appeared in Palestine (principally the Canaanite, Philistine, [Hebraic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrews), [Nabatean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabatean), Syrio-Aramaic and Arab).[^cite_note-Tamari-265]

Palestinian culture is closely related to those of the nearby Levantine countries such as Lebanon, Syria, and Jordan, and the Arab World. Cultural contributions to the fields of [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), [costume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costume) and [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine) express the characteristics of the Palestinian experience and show signs of common origin despite the geographical separation between the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories), Israel and the diaspora.[^cite_note-Elmokadem-266][^cite_note-Moran-267][^cite_note-268]&#x20;

[Al-Quds Capital of Arab Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Quds_Capital_of_Arab_Culture) is an initiative undertaken by [UNESCO](21786641.md) under the [Cultural Capitals Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Capital_of_Culture) to promote Arab culture and encourage cooperation in the Arab region. The opening event was launched in March 2009.&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Palestinian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_cuisine)



Palestinian market at [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa), 1877 painting

Palestine's history of rule by many different empires is reflected in Palestinian cuisine, which has benefited from various cultural contributions and exchanges. Generally speaking, modern Syrian-Palestinian dishes have been influenced by the rule of three major Islamic groups: the Arabs, the [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_cuisine)-influenced Arabs and the [Turks](11125639.md).[^cite_note-CDN-269] The Arabs who conquered Syria and Palestine had simple culinary traditions primarily based on the use of rice, lamb and yogurt, as well as dates.[^cite_note-ArabNet-270] The already simple cuisine did not advance for centuries due to [Islam](6037917.md)'s strict rules of parsimony and restraint, until the rise of the [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid), who established [Baghdad](4492.md) as their capital. Baghdad was historically located on Persian soil and henceforth, Persian culture was integrated into Arab culture during the 9th–11th centuries and spread throughout central areas of the empire.[^cite_note-CDN-269]&#x20;

There are several foods native to Palestine that are well known in the Arab world, such as, *[kinafe Nabulsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanafeh)*, [Nabulsi cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabulsi_cheese) (cheese of [Nablus](233369.md)), [Ackawi cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ackawi_cheese) (cheese of [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel)) and *[musakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musakhan)*. *Kinafe* originated in Nablus, as well as the sweetened *Nabulsi* cheese used to fill it. Another very popular food is Palestinian Kofta or Kufta.[^cite_note-271]&#x20;

[Mezze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezze) describes an assortment of dishes laid out on the table for a meal that takes place over several hours, a characteristic common to [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean) cultures. Some common mezze dishes are *[hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus)*, *[tabouleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabouleh)*,*[baba ghanoush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_ghanoush)*, *[labaneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labaneh)*, and *zate 'u [zaatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaatar)*, which is the pita bread dipping of olive oil and ground [thyme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyme) and [sesame seeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame_seeds).[^cite_note-272]&#x20;

[Entrées](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entr%C3%A9e) that are eaten throughout the Palestinian territories, include *[waraq al-'inib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma)* – boiled [grape leaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grape_leaves) wrapped around cooked [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) and ground [lamb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_sheep). *Mahashi* is an assortment of stuffed vegetables such as, zucchinis, potatoes, cabbage and in Gaza, chard.[^cite_note-273]&#x20;

### Art

Main article: [Palestinian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_art)



The [Umm al-Fahm Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_al-Fahm_Art_Gallery)

Similar to the structure of Palestinian society, the Palestinian field of arts extends over four main geographic centers: the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md), [Israel](9282173.md), the [Palestinian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_diaspora) in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), and the Palestinian diaspora in [Europe](9239.md), the [United States](3434750.md) and elsewhere.[^cite_note-Zvi-274]&#x20;

Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Palestine)

Palestinian cinematography, relatively young compared to [Arab cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cinema) overall, receives much European and Israeli support.[^cite_note-275] Palestinian films are not exclusively produced in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic); some are made in English, French or Hebrew.[^cite_note-276] More than 800 films have been produced about Palestinians, the Israeli–Palestinian conflict, and other related topics.[^cite_note-277] Examples include *[Divine Intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Intervention_\(2002_film\))* and *[Paradise Now](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradise_Now)*.&#x20;

- The Alhamra Cinema, Jaffa, 1937, bombed December 1947

  The Alhamra Cinema, [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa), 1937, [bombed December 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Irgun_attacks)

- Villagers in Halhul at an open-air cinema screening c. 1940

  Villagers in [Halhul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halhul) at an open-air cinema screening c. 1940

Handicrafts

Main article: [Palestinian handicrafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_handicrafts)

A wide variety of handicrafts, many of which have been produced in the area of Palestine for hundreds of years, continue to be produced today. Palestinian handicrafts include [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_costumes#Palestinian_embroidery) and weaving, [pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_pottery)-making, [soap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabulsi_soap)-making, [glass-making](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebron_glass), and [olive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive)-wood and [Mother of Pearl carvings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother-of-Pearl_carving_in_Bethlehem), among others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobsEberSilvani199872-278][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarmi200518-279]&#x20;

Traditional costumes

Main article: [Palestinian costumes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_costumes)

Foreign travelers to Palestine in the late 19th and early 20th centuries often commented on the rich variety of costumes among the area's inhabitants, and particularly among the [fellaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fellaheen) or village women. Until the 1940s, a woman's economic status, whether married or single, and the town or area they were from could be deciphered by most Palestinian women by the type of cloth, colors, cut, and [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery) motifs, or lack thereof, used for the robe-like dress or "thoub" in Arabic.[^cite_note-Aramco-280]&#x20;

New styles began to appear in the 1960s. For example, the "six-branched dress" named after the six wide bands of embroidery running down from the waist.[^cite_note-281] These styles came from the refugee camps, particularly after 1967. Individual village styles were lost and replaced by an identifiable "Palestinian" style.[^cite_note-282] The shawal, a style popular in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Jordan](7515964.md) before the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada), probably evolved from one of the many [welfare](46539.md) embroidery projects in the [refugee camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_refugee_camps). It was a shorter and narrower fashion, with a western cut.[^cite_note-283]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Palestinian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_literature)



Palestinian novelist and non-fiction writer [Susan Abulhawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Abulhawa)



[Mahmoud Darwish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Darwish), Palestinian poet

Palestinian literature forms part of the wider genre of [Arabic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_literature). Unlike its Arabic counterparts, Palestinian literature is defined by national affiliation rather than territorially. For example, Egyptian literature is the literature produced in Egypt. This too was the case for Palestinian literature up to the [1948 Arab-Israeli war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab-Israeli_war), but following the [Palestinian Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_exodus) of 1948 it has become "a literature written by Palestinians" regardless of their residential status.[^cite_note-Kochavi-284][^cite_note-Suleiman2006-285]&#x20;

Contemporary Palestinian literature is often characterized by its heightened sense of [irony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irony) and the exploration of existential themes and issues of identity.[^cite_note-Suleiman2006-285] References to the subjects of resistance to occupation, [exile](9855.md), loss, and love and longing for [homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland) are also common.[^cite_note-286] Palestinian literature can be intensely political, as underlined by writers such as [Salma Khadra Jayyusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salma_Khadra_Jayyusi) and novelist [Liana Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liana_Badr), who have mentioned the need to give expression to the Palestinian "collective identity" and the "just case" of their struggle.[^cite_note-Soueif-287] There is also resistance to this school of thought, whereby Palestinian artists have "rebelled" against the demand that their art be "committed".[^cite_note-Soueif-287] Poet [Mourid Barghouti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mourid_Barghouti) for example, has often said that "poetry is not a civil servant, it's not a soldier, it's in nobody's employ."[^cite_note-Soueif-287] [Rula Jebreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rula_Jebreal)'s novel *[Miral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miral)* tells the story of [Hind al-Husseini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hind_al-Husseini)'s effort to establish an [orphanage](387703.md) in [Jerusalem](16043.md) after the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War), the [Deir Yassin massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_Yassin_massacre),[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] and the establishment of the [State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel).&#x20;

Since 1967, most critics have theorized the existence of three "branches" of Palestinian literature, loosely divided by geographic location: 1) from inside Israel, 2) from the [occupied territories](2781576.md), 3) from among the [Palestinian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_diaspora) throughout the [Middle East](19323.md).[^cite_note-Salaita-290]&#x20;

Hannah Amit-Kochavi recognizes only two branches: that written by Palestinians from inside the State of Israel as distinct from that written outside (ibid., p. 11).[^cite_note-Kochavi-284] She also posits a temporal distinction between literature produced before 1948 and that produced thereafter.[^cite_note-Kochavi-284] In a 2003 article published in *Studies in the Humanities*, Steven Salaita posits a fourth branch made up of [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) works, particularly those written by Palestinians in the [United States](3434750.md), which he defines as "writing rooted in diasporic countries but focused in theme and content on [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\))."[^cite_note-Salaita-290]&#x20;



[Palestinian-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian-American) writer [Naomi Shihab Nye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naomi_Shihab_Nye)

Poetry, using classical pre-Islamic forms, remains an extremely popular art form, often attracting Palestinian audiences in the thousands. Until 20 years ago, local folk bards reciting traditional verses were a feature of every Palestinian town.[^cite_note-Shahin41-291] After the 1948 Palestinian exodus and discrimination by neighboring Arab countries, poetry was transformed into a vehicle for political activism.[^cite_note-Caplan-171] From among those Palestinians who became [Arab citizens of Israel](492331.md) after the passage of the Citizenship Law in 1952, a school of resistance poetry was born that included poets including [Mahmoud Darwish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Darwish), [Samih al-Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samih_al-Qasim), and [Tawfiq Zayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfiq_Zayyad).[^cite_note-Shahin41-291] The work of these poets was largely unknown to the wider Arab world for years because of the lack of diplomatic relations between Israel and Arab governments. The situation changed after [Ghassan Kanafani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassan_Kanafani), another Palestinian writer in exile in Lebanon, published an anthology of their work in 1966.[^cite_note-Shahin41-291] Palestinian poets often write about the common theme of a strong affection and sense of loss and longing for a lost homeland.[^cite_note-Shahin41-291] Among the new generation of Palestinian writers, the work of [Nathalie Handal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathalie_Handal) an award-winning poet, playwright, and editor has been widely published in literary journals and magazines and has been translated into twelve languages.[^cite_note-292]&#x20;



[Samah Sabawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samah_Sabawi) is a Palestinian dramatist, writer and journalist.

Palestinian folklore is the body of expressive culture, including tales, [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance), [legends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legend), [oral history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition), [proverbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proverb), jokes, popular beliefs, [customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tradition), and comprising the traditions (including oral traditions) of Palestinian culture. There was a folklorist revival among Palestinian intellectuals such as Nimr Sirhan, Musa Allush, Salim Mubayyid, and the Palestinian [Folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore) Society during the 1970s. This group attempted to establish pre-Islamic (and pre-Hebraic) cultural roots for a re-constructed Palestinian national identity. The two putative roots in this patrimony are Canaanite and Jebusite.[^cite_note-Tamari-265] Such efforts seem to have borne fruit as evidenced in the organization of celebrations including the [Qabatiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qabatiya) Canaanite festival and the annual Music Festival of [Yabus](16043.md) by the Palestinian Ministry of Culture.[^cite_note-Tamari-265]&#x20;

Traditional storytelling among Palestinians is prefaced with an invitation to the listeners to give blessings to God and the Prophet Mohammed or the Virgin Mary as the case may be, and includes the traditional opening: "There was, or there was not, in the oldness of time..."[^cite_note-Shahin41-291][^cite_note-Muhawi-293] Formulaic elements of the stories share much in common with the wider Arab world, though the rhyming scheme is distinct. There are a cast of supernatural characters: [djinns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinns) who can cross the Seven Seas in an instant, giants, and ghouls with eyes of ember and teeth of brass. Stories invariably have a happy ending, and the storyteller will usually finish off with a rhyme like: "The bird has taken flight, God bless you tonight", or "Tutu, tutu, finished is my *haduttu* (story)."[^cite_note-Shahin41-291]&#x20;

### Music



[Kamanjeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamanjah) performer in Jerusalem, 1859

[^cite_note-294]

[Palestinian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_music) is well known throughout the Arab world.[^cite_note-Poche-295] After 1948, a new wave of performers emerged with distinctively Palestinian themes relating to dreams of statehood and burgeoning nationalist sentiments. In addition to *[zajal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zajal)* and *[ataaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ataaba)*, traditional Palestinian songs include: *Bein Al-dawai*, *Al-Rozana*, *Zarif – Al-Toul*, and *Al-Maijana*, *Dal'ona*, *Sahja/Saamir*, *Zaghareet*. Over three decades, the Palestinian National Music and Dance Troupe (El Funoun) and [Mohsen Subhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohsen_Subhi) have reinterpreted and rearranged traditional wedding songs such as *Mish'al* (1986), *Marj Ibn 'Amer*(1989) and *Zaghareed* (1997).[^cite_note-296] *Ataaba* is a form of folk singing that consists of four verses, following a specific form and meter. The distinguishing feature of ataaba is that the first three verses end with the same word meaning three different things, and the fourth verse serves as a conclusion. It is usually followed by a *[dalouna](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dalouna\&action=edit\&redlink=1)*.&#x20;

[Reem Kelani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reem_Kelani) is one of the foremost researchers and performers in the present day of music with a specifically Palestinian narrative and heritage.[^cite_note-297] Her 2006 debut solo album *Sprinting Gazelle – Palestinian Songs from the Motherland and the Diaspora* comprised Kelani's research and an arrangement of five traditional Palestinian songs, whilst the other five songs were her own musical settings of popular and resistance poetry by the likes of Mahmoud Darwish, [Salma Khadra Jayyusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salma_Khadra_Jayyusi), [Rashid Husain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Hussein) and Mahmoud Salim al-Hout.[^cite_note-298] All the songs on the album relate to 'pre-1948 Palestine'.&#x20;

#### Palestinian hip hop

Main article: [Palestinian hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_hip_hop)

[Palestinian hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_hip_hop) reportedly started in 1998 with [Tamer Nafar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamer_Nafar)'s group [DAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAM_\(band\)).[^cite_note-299] These Palestinian youth forged the new Palestinian musical subgenre, which blends [Arabic melodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music) and [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop) beats. Lyrics are often sung in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), English, and sometimes French. Since then, the new Palestinian musical subgenre has grown to include artists in the Palestinian territories, Israel, Great Britain, the United States and Canada.&#x20;



[Saint Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Levant) is a Palestinian singer-songwriter and rapper

Borrowing from [traditional rap music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_school_hip-hop) that first emerged in New York in the 1970s, "young Palestinian musicians have tailored the style to express their own grievances with the social and political climate in which they live and work." Palestinian hip hop works to challenge [stereotypes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotype) and instigate dialogue about the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md).[^cite_note-300] Palestinian hip-hop artists have been strongly influenced by the messages of American rappers. Tamar Nafar says, "When I heard Tupac sing 'It's a White Man's World' I decided to take hip hop seriously".[^cite_note-Maira-301] In addition to the influences from American hip hop, it also includes musical elements from Palestinian and Arabic music including "zajal, mawwal, and saj" which can be likened to Arabic spoken word, as well as including the percussiveness and lyricism of Arabic music.&#x20;

Historically, music has served as an integral accompaniment to various social and religious rituals and ceremonies in Palestinian society (Al-Taee 47). Much of the Middle-Eastern and Arabic string instruments utilized in classical Palestinian music are sampled over Hip-hop beats in both Israeli and Palestinian hip-hop as part of a joint process of localization. Just as the percussiveness of the Hebrew language is emphasized in Israeli Hip-hop, Palestinian music has always revolved around the rhythmic specificity and smooth melodic tone of Arabic. "Musically speaking, Palestinian songs are usually pure melody performed monophonically with complex vocal ornamentations and strong percussive rhythm beats".[^cite_note-302] The presence of a hand-drum in classical Palestinian music indicates a cultural esthetic conducive to the vocal, verbal and instrumental percussion which serve as the foundational elements of Hip-hop. This hip hop is joining a "longer tradition of revolutionary, underground, Arabic music and political songs that have supported Palestinian Resistance".[^cite_note-Maira-301] This subgenre has served as a way to politicize the Palestinian issue through music.&#x20;

#### Dance

The [Dabke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabke), a Levantine Arab folk dance style whose local Palestinian versions were appropriated by Palestinian nationalism after [1967](29328.md), has, according to one scholar, possible roots that may go back to ancient [Canaanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan) fertility rites.[^cite_note-Canaan-303] It is marked by synchronized jumping, stamping, and movement, similar to tap dancing. One version is performed by men, another by women.&#x20;

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Palestine)

Although sport facilities did exist before the [1948 Palestinian expulsion and flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight), many such facilities and institutions were subsequently shut down. Today there remains sport centers such as in Gaza and Ramallah, but the difficulty of mobility and travel restrictions means most Palestinian are not able to compete internationally to their full potential. However, Palestinian sport authorities have indicated that Palestinians in the diaspora will be eligible to compete for Palestine once the diplomatic and security situation improves.&#x20;

[^cite_note-indigenous-32]: &#x20; [Dowty 2023](#CITEREFDowty2023), 3. The Arab Story to 1914: "Palestine was part of the first wave of conquest following Muhammad's death in 632 CE; Jerusalem fell to the Caliph Umar in 638. The indigenous population, descended from Jews, other Semitic groups, and non-Semitic groups such as the Philistines, had been mostly Christianized. Over succeeding centuries it was Islamicized, and Arabic replaced Aramaic (a Semitic tongue closely related to Hebrew) as the dominant language" [Dowty 2023](#CITEREFDowty2023), 10. The Perfect Conflict: "Palestinians are the descendants of all the indigenous peoples who lived in Palestine over the centuries; since the seventh century, they have been predominantly Muslim in religion and almost completely Arab in language and culture." [Gelvin 2021](#CITEREFGelvin2021), p. 100: "Furthermore, Zionism itself was also defined by its opposition to the indigenous Palestinian inhabitants of the region. Both the 'conquest of land' and the 'conquest of labor' slogans that became central to the dominant strain of Zionism in the Yishuv originated as a result of the Zionist confrontation with the Palestinian 'other'." [Danver 2015](#CITEREFDanver2015), p. 554: "The origin of the term Palestinian is uncertain. Some historians connect it to the Philistines, a biblical people that resided on the eastern coast of the Mediterranean Sea as early as the twelfth century B.C.E. Thus, Palestinians are considered by some to be the indigenous people of present-day Israel, the West Bank, and the Gaza Strip. Other scholars dispute this view, asserting that Jews and others resided in Palestine—usually defined as the narrow strip of land bordered by the Jordan River and the Mediterranean Sea—long before these Arabs arrived in the seventh century." [Esposito 2004](#CITEREFEsposito2004), Arab-Israeli Conflict: "Although their leaders welcomed the Jews as refugees, many Palestinians (the indigenous Arab population of Palestine) viewed the arrival of Jewish settlers as a threat to their security and to their land." [Falk 2017](#CITEREFFalk2017), pp. 14, 201: "Although at the beginning of the twentieth century a considerable proportion of the Palestinian Arabs were evidently immigrants from adjacent countries, some may be the descendents of the ancient inhabitants of the country. Thus, some Palestinian Arabs, like the Jews, may claim a genetic relationship to the ancient inhabitants of the country."; "Even though not a race in a biological sense, political Zionism, after a century of attempts to prove contemporary Jews' material, biological relationships – not merely their spiritual, cultural ones – to the ancient people of the biblical stories, in spite of widespread interspersing with local communities, finally has succeeded. It is tragic that Zionism, as well as Arab Nationalism, have failed to recognize the Palestinians, many of whom similarly appear to share phylogenetic relations to the historic inhabitants of the country, as equal partners." Tanous, Osama; Asi, Yara; Hammoudeh, Weeam; Mills, David; Wispelwey, Bram (2023). ["Structural racism and the health of Palestinian citizens of Israel"](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F17441692.2023.2214608). *[Global Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Public_Health)*. **18** (1) 2214608. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1080/17441692.2023.2214608](https://doi.org/10.1080%2F17441692.2023.2214608). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [37209155](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37209155). On the other hand, Palestinians, the vast majority and indigenous inhabitants of the land, were negatively defined by what they were not; i.e. as non-Jewish communities with civil and religious rights, but not political or territorial rights to the land

[^cite_note-33]: &#x20; [Nassar 2019](#CITEREFNassar2019): "The emergence of Palestinians as a national group \[...] cannot be solely based on biological roots or continuous habitation of their homeland, but primarily on when, and how they began to imagine and articulate themselves as belonging to a national group. At the same time, such imagination ought to be considered within the historical context in which it developed. In other words, the fact that the people whom we call Palestinians today are the descendants of the various groups that inhabited Palestine throughout its history is not enough to unpack the history of their national identity." [Jabareen 2002](#CITEREFJabareen2002), p. 214: "This blurring has led to a situation in which characteristics of the State of Israel are presented as characteristics of a nation-state, even though (de facto) it is a binational state, and Palestinian citizens are presented as an ethnic minority group although they are a homeland majority." [Hussain & Shumock 2006](#CITEREFHussainShumock2006), p. 269ff, 284: "The Palestinians...are an ethnic minority in their country of residence." [Nasser 2013](#CITEREFNasser2013), p. 69: "What is noteworthy here is the use of a general category 'Arabs', instead of a more specific one of 'Palestinians.' By turning to a general category, the particularity of Palestinians, among other ethnic and national groups, is erased and in its place Jordanian identity is implanted." [Haklai 2011](#CITEREFHaklai2011), p. 112: "...throughout the 1990s and 2000s a growing number of PAI political organizations have been increasingly promoting Palestinian consciousness, advancing ethnonationalist objectives, and demanding recognition of collective group rights." Abu-Rayya, Hisham Motkal; Abu-Rayya, Maram Hussien (2009). [Yakobson & Rubinstein 2009](#CITEREFYakobsonRubinstein2009), p. 179: "Of course, the notion that the Palestinians are an Arab people, an integral part of the Arab world ('the Arab nation'), is wholly legitimate and natural, given the history and culture of the people in question." [Wilmer 2021](#CITEREFWilmer2021), p. 14: "People know who they are, where they live, and where their families have lived for centuries or millennia." [Abu-Libdeh, Turnpenny & Teebi 2012](#CITEREFAbu-LibdehTurnpennyTeebi2012), p. 700: "Palestinians are an indigenous people who either live in, or originate from, historical Palestine.... Although the Muslims guaranteed security and allowed religious freedom to all inhabitants of the region, the majority converted to Islam and adopted Arab culture." [Encyclopedia Britannica](#CITEREFEncyclopedia_Britannica), [From the Arab conquest to 1900](https://www.britannica.com/place/Palestine/Roman-Palestine#ref45060): "The process of Arabization and Islamization was gaining momentum there. It was one of the mainstays of Umayyad power and was important in their struggle against both Iraq and the Arabian Peninsula.... Conversions arising from convenience as well as conviction then increased. These conversions to Islam, together with a steady tribal inflow from the desert, changed the religious character of Palestine's inhabitants. The predominantly Christian population gradually became predominantly Muslim and Arabic-speaking. At the same time, during the early years of Muslim control of the city, a small permanent Jewish population returned to [Jerusalem](16043.md) after a 500-year absence." [Lewis 1999](#CITEREFLewis1999), p. 169 [Parkes 1970](#CITEREFParkes1970), pp. 209–10: "the word 'Arab' needs to be used with care. It is applicable to the Bedouin and to a section of the urban and effendi classes; it is inappropriate as a description of the rural mass of the population, the fellaheen. The whole population spoke Arabic, usually corrupted by dialects bearing traces of words of other origin, but it was only the Bedouin who habitually thought of themselves as Arabs. Western travelers from the sixteenth century onwards make the same distinction, and the word 'Arab' almost always refers to them exclusively.... Gradually it was realized that there remained a substantial stratum of the pre-Israelite peasantry, and that the oldest element among the peasants were not 'Arabs' in the sense of having entered the country with or after the conquerors of the seventh century, had been there already when the Arabs came."

[^cite_note-Gelvin2014-42]: &#x20;Gelvin, James L. (2014). [*The Israel-Palestine Conflict: One Hundred Years of War*](https://books.google.com/books?id=GDaZAgAAQBAJ\&pg=PA93) (3 ed.). Cambridge University Press. p. 93. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-107-47077-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-107-47077-4). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20231129192547/https://books.google.com/books?id=GDaZAgAAQBAJ\&pg=PA93#v=onepage\&q\&f=false) from the original on 29 November 2023. Retrieved 29 November 2023. Palestinian nationalism emerged during the interwar period in response to Zionist immigration and settlement. The fact that Palestinian nationalism developed later than Zionism and indeed in response to it does not in any way diminish the legitimacy of Palestinian nationalism or make it less valid than Zionism. All nationalisms arise in opposition to some "other". Why else would there be the need to specify who you are? And all nationalisms are defined by what they oppose. As we have seen, Zionism itself arose in reaction to anti-Semitic and exclusionary nationalist movements in Europe. It would be perverse to judge Zionism as somehow less valid than European anti-Semitism or those nationalisms. . . Furthermore, Zionism itself was also defined by its opposition to the indigenous Palestinian inhabitants of the region. Both the "conquest of land" and the "conquest of labor" slogans that became central to the dominant strain of Zionism in the Yishuv originated as a result of the Zionist confrontation with the Palestinian "other".

[^cite_note-AK2013-61]: &#x20;Killebrew, Ann E. (2013), ["The Philistines and Other "Sea Peoples" in Text and Archaeology"](https://books.google.com/books?id=gBCl2IQfNioC\&pg=PA1), *Society of Biblical Literature Archaeology and biblical studies*, vol. 15, Society of Biblical Lit, p. 2, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-58983-721-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-58983-721-8), [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20231129192547/https://books.google.com/books?id=gBCl2IQfNioC\&pg=PA1#v=onepage\&q\&f=false) from the original on 29 November 2023, retrieved 29 November 2023. Quote: "First coined in 1881 by the French Egyptologist G. Maspero (1896), the somewhat misleading term "Sea Peoples" encompasses the ethnonyms Lukka, Sherden, Shekelesh, Teresh, Eqwesh, Denyen, Sikil / Tjekker, Weshesh, and Peleset (Philistines). \[Footnote: The modern term "Sea Peoples" refers to peoples that appear in several New Kingdom Egyptian texts as originating from "islands" (tables 1–2; Adams and Cohen, this volume; see, e.g., [Drews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Drews) 1993, 57 for a summary). The use of quotation marks in association with the term "Sea Peoples" in our title is intended to draw attention to the problematic nature of this commonly used term. It is noteworthy that the designation "of the sea" appears only in relation to the Sherden, Shekelesh, and Eqwesh. Subsequently, this term was applied somewhat indiscriminately to several additional ethnonyms, including the Philistines, who are portrayed in their earliest appearance as invaders from the north during the reigns of Merenptah and Ramesses Ill (see, e.g., Sandars 1978; Redford 1992, 243, n. 14; for a recent review of the primary and secondary literature, see Woudhuizen 2006). Hencefore the term Sea Peoples will appear without quotation marks.]

[^cite_note-Drews48-62]: &#x20;Drews, Robert (1993). [*The End of the Bronze Age: Changes in Warfare and the Catastrophe Ca. 1200 B.C.*](https://books.google.com/books?id=bFpK6aXEWN8C\&pg=PA48) Princeton University Press. pp. 48–61. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-691-02591-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-691-02591-6). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20231129192547/https://books.google.com/books?id=bFpK6aXEWN8C\&pg=PA48#v=onepage\&q\&f=false) from the original on 29 November 2023. The thesis that a great "migration of the Sea Peoples" occurred ca. 1200 B.C. is supposedly based on Egyptian inscriptions, one from the reign of Merneptah and another from the reign of Ramesses III. Yet in the inscriptions themselves such a migration nowhere appears. After reviewing what the Egyptian texts have to say about 'the sea peoples', one Egyptologist (Wolfgang Helck) recently remarked that although some things are unclear, "eins ist aber sicher: Nach den ägyptischen Texten haben wir es nicht mit einer "Völkerwanderung" zu tun." ("one thing is clear: according to the Egyptian texts, we are not dealing here with a '[Völkerwanderung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_Period)' \[migration of peoples as in 4th–6th-century Europe].") Thus the migration hypothesis is based not on the inscriptions themselves but on their interpretation.

BRILL. pp. 301–363. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-04-17729-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-04-17729-1). [^cite_note-H.H._Ben-Sasson,_1976,_page_334-64]: &#x20;Ben-Sasson, H.H. (1976). *A History of the Jewish People*. Harvard University Press. p. 334. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-674-39731-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-674-39731-2). In an effort to wipe out all memory of the bond between the Jews and the land, Hadrian changed the name of the province from Judaea to Syria-Palestina, a name that became common in non-Jewish literature.

[^cite_note-Ariel_Lewin_p._33-65]: &#x20;Lewin, Ariel (2005). *The archaeology of Ancient Judea and Palestine*. Getty Publications. p. 33. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-89236-800-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-89236-800-4). It seems clear that by choosing a seemingly neutral name – one juxtaposing that of a neighboring province with the revived name of an ancient geographical entity (Palestine), already known from the writings of Herodotus – Hadrian was intending to suppress any connection between the Jewish people and that land.

[^cite_note-F90-66]: &#x20;[Feldman 1990](#CITEREFFeldman1990), p. 19: "While it is true that there is no evidence as to precisely who changed the name of Judaea to Palestine and precisely when this was done, circumstantial evidence would seem to point to Hadrian himself, since he is, it would seem, responsible for a number of decrees that sought to crush the national and religious spirit of thejews, whether these decrees were responsible for the uprising or were the result of it. In the first place, he refounded Jerusalem as a Graeco-Roman city under the name of Aelia Capitolina. He also erected on the site of the Temple another temple to Zeus."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacobson200144–45-67]: &#x20;[Jacobson 2001](#CITEREFJacobson2001), pp. 44–45: "Hadrian officially renamed Judea Syria Palaestina after his Roman armies suppressed the Bar-Kokhba Revolt (the Second Jewish Revolt) in 135 C.E.; this is commonly viewed as a move intended to sever the connection of the Jews to their historical homeland. However, that Jewish writers such as Philo, in particular, and Josephus, who flourished while Judea was still formally in existence, used the name Palestine for the Land of Israel in their Greek works, suggests that this interpretation of history is mistaken. Hadrian's choice of Syria Palaestina may be more correctly seen as a rationalization of the name of the new province, in accordance with its area being far larger than geographical Judea. Indeed, Syria Palaestina had an ancient pedigree that was intimately linked with the area of greater Israel."

Despite the long regnant model that the Canaanites and Israelites were people of fundamentally different culture, archaeological data now casts doubt on this view. The material culture of the region exhibits numerous common points between Israelites and Canaanites in the Iron I period (c. 1200–1000 BCE). The record would suggest that the Israelite culture largely overlapped with and derived from Canaanite culture... In short, Israelite culture was largely Canaanite in nature. Given the information available, one cannot maintain a radical cultural separation between Canaanites and Israelites for the Iron I period.

[^cite_note-:5-101]: &#x20;Ehrlich, Michael (2022). *The Islamization of the Holy Land, 634–1800*. Leeds, UK: Arc Humanities Press. pp. 3–4. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-64189-222-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-64189-222-3). Samaritan rebellions during the fifth and sixth centuries were crushed by the Byzantines and as a result, the main Samaritan communities began to decline. Similarly, the Jewish community strove to recover from the catastrophic results of the Bar Kokhva revolt (132–135 ce). During the Late Roman and Byzantine periods, many Jews emigrated to thriving centres in the diaspora, especially Iraq, whereas some converted to Christianity and others continued to live in the Holy Land, especially in Galilee and the coastal plain. \[...] Accordingly, most of the Muslims who participated in the conquest of the Holy Land did not settle there, but continued on to further destinations. For most of the Muslims who settled in the Holy Land were either Arabs who immigrated before the Muslim conquest and then converted to Islam, or Muslims who immigrated after the Holy Land's conquest. \[...] Consequently, many local Christians converted to Islam. Thus, almost twelve centuries later, when the army led by Napoleon Bonaparte arrived in the Holy Land, most of the local population was Muslim. \[...] The Holy Land's transformation from an area populated mainly by Christians into a region whose population was predominantly Muslim was the result of two processes: immigration and conversion

[^cite_note-102]: &#x20;Bar, Doron (2003). "The Christianisation of Rural Palestine during Late Antiquity". *The Journal of Ecclesiastical History*. **54** (3): 401–421. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/s0022046903007309](https://doi.org/10.1017%2Fs0022046903007309). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0022-0469](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0022-0469). The dominant view of the history of Palestine during the Byzantine period links the early phases of the consecration of the land during the fourth century and the substantial external financial investment that accompanied the building of churches on holy sites on the one hand with the Christianisation of the population on the other. Churches were erected primarily at the holy sites, 12 while at the same time Palestine's position and unique status as the Christian 'Holy Land' became more firmly rooted. All this, coupled with immigration and conversion, allegedly meant that the Christianisation of Palestine took place much more rapidly than that of other areas of the Roman empire, brought in its wake the annihilation of the pagan cults and meant that by the middle of the fifth century there was a clear Christian majority.

[^cite_note-:142-103]: &#x20;Ehrlich, Michael (2022). *The Islamization of the Holy Land, 634–1800*. Arc Humanities Press. pp. 3–4. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-64189-222-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-64189-222-3). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [1302180905](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/1302180905). The Jewish community strove to recover from the catastrophic results of the Bar Kokhva revolt (132–135 CE). Although some of these attempts were relatively successful, the Jews never fully recovered. During the Late Roman and Byzantine periods, many Jews emigrated to thriving centres in the diaspora, especially Iraq, whereas some converted to Christianity and others continued to live in the Holy Land, especially in Galilee and the coastal plain. During the Byzantine period, the three provinces of Palestine included more than thirty cities, namely, settlements with a bishop see. After the Muslim conquest in the 630s, most of these cities declined and eventually disappeared. As a result, in many cases the local ecclesiastical administration weakened, while in others it simply ceased to exist. Consequently, many local Christians converted to Islam. Thus, almost twelve centuries later, when the army led by Napoleon Bonaparte arrived in the Holy Land, most of the local population was Muslim.

[*Distribution and Population Density During the Late Ottoman and Early Mandate Periods*](https://www.taylorfrancis.com/books/mono/10.4324/9781315128825/rural-arab-demography-early-jewish-settlement-palestine-david-grossman) (9781315128825 ed.). New York: [Routledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge). pp. 44–52. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4324/9781315128825](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781315128825). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-315-12882-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-315-12882-5). Retrieved 29 November 2023. They came from Circassia and Chechnya, and were refugees from territories annexed by Russia in 1864, and the Bosnian Muslims, whose province was lost to Serbia in 1878. Belonging to this category were the Algerians (Mughrabis), who arrived in Syria and Palestine in several waves after 1850 in the wake of France's conquest of their country and the waves of Egyptian migration to Palestine and Syria during the rule of Muhammad Ali and his son, Ibrahim Pasha. \[...] In most cases the Egyptian army dropouts and the other Egyptian settlers preferred to settle in existing localities, rather than to establish new villages. In the southern coastal plain and Ramla zones there were at least nineteen villages which had families of Egyptian origin, and in the northern part of Samaria, including the 'Ara Valley, there are a number of villages with substantial population of Egyptian stock.

[^cite_note-:FrantzmanKark20133-117]: &#x20;Frantzman, Seth J.; Kark, Ruth (16 April 2013). "The Muslim Settlement of Late Ottoman and Mandatory Palestine: Comparison with Jewish Settlement Patterns". *Digest of Middle East Studies*. **22** (1): 77. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1111/j.1949-3606.2012.00172.x](https://doi.org/10.1111%2Fj.1949-3606.2012.00172.x). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [1060-4367](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1060-4367). Some of these Muslims were Egyptian and Algerian immigrants who came to Palestine in the first half of the nineteenth century from foreign lands. There were also Algerians, Bosnians, and Circassians, who came in the second half of the nineteenth century, but most were from within the borders of Palestine.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrantzmanKark201389-118]: &#x20;[Frantzman & Kark 2013](#CITEREFFrantzmanKark2013), p. 89.

[^cite_note-:73-119]: &#x20;Swedenburg, Ted (2003). [*Memories of Revolt: The 1936–1939 Rebellion and the Palestinian National Past*](https://books.google.com/books?id=q7RTdcvtO2sC\&pg=PA81). University of Arkansas Press. p. 81. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-55728-763-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-55728-763-2). These primordialist claims regarding the Palestinians' primeval and prior roots in the land operated at the level of the collective. When it came to an individual's own family, however, Arab-Islamic discourse took precedence over archaeological justifications. I ran across no Palestinian villager (or urbanite) who claimed personal descent from the Canaanites. Villagers typically traced their family or their hamila's origins back to a more recent past in the Arabian peninsula. Many avowed descent from some nomadic tribe that had migrated from Arabia to Palestine either during or shortly after the Arab-Islamic conquests. By such a claim they inserted their family's history into the narrative of Arab and Islamic civilization and connected themselves to a genealogy that possessed greater local and contemporary prestige than did ancient or pre-Islamic descent. Several men specifically connected their forefathers' date of entry into Palestine to their participation in the army of Salih al-Din al-Ayyubi (Saladin), a historical figure whose significance has been retrospectively enlarged by nationalist discourse such that he is now regarded not merely as a hero of "Islamic" civilization but as a "national" luminary as well.+ (Modern nationalist discourse tends to downplay Salah al-Din's Kurdish origins.) Palestinians of all political stripes viewed Salah al-Din's wars against the Crusaders as a forerunner of the current combats against foreign intruders. Many considered Salah al-Din's victory over the Crusaders at Hittin (A.D. 1187) as a historical precedent that offered hope for their own eventual triumph even if, like the Crusader wars, the current struggle with Israel was destined to last more than two centuries. Family histories affiliated to earlier "patriotic" struggles against European aggression tied interviewees to a continuous narrative of national resistance. Villagers claiming descent from Arabs who entered Palestine during the Arab-Islamic conquest equally viewed these origins as establishing their historical precedence over the Jews

["Interview With Middle East Scholar Avi Shlaim: America, Israel and the Middle East"](https://web.archive.org/web/20191113024502/https://www.thenation.com/article/interview-middle-east-scholar-avi-shlaim/). *The Nation*. Archived from [the original](http://www.thenation.com/article/interview-middle-east-scholar-avi-shlaim) on 13 November 2019. Retrieved 9 March 2008.

[^cite_note-ICJ-157]: &#x20;Only "peoples" are entitled to self-determination in contemporary international law (See Self-determination and National Minorities, Oxford Monographs in International Law, Thomas D. Musgrave, Oxford University Press, 1997, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0-19-829898-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0-19-829898-6), p. 170). In 2004, the International Court of Justice said that Israel had recognized the existence of a "Palestinian people" and referred a number of times to the Palestinian people and its "legitimate rights" in international agreements. The Court said those rights include the right to self-determination(See paragraph 118 of Legal Consequences of the Construction of a Wall in the Occupied Palestinian Territory ["Cour internationale de Justice – International Court of Justice | International Court of Justice"](https://web.archive.org/web/20100706021237/http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1671.pdf) (PDF). Archived from [the original](http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1671.pdf) (PDF) on 6 July 2010. Retrieved 6 July 2010.). Judge Koroma explained "The Court has also held that the right of self-determination as an established and recognized right under international law applies to the territory and to the Palestinian people. Accordingly, the exercise of such right entitles the Palestinian people to a State of their own as originally envisaged in resolution 181 (II) and subsequently confirmed." Judge Higgins also said "that the Palestinian people are entitled to their territory, to exercise self-determination, and to have their own State"(See paragraph 5, Separate opinion of Judge Koroma ["Cour internationale de Justice – International Court of Justice | International Court of Justice"](https://web.archive.org/web/20110604233639/http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1679.pdf) (PDF). Archived from [the original](http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1679.pdf) (PDF) on 4 June 2011. Retrieved 7 February 2010. and paragraph 18, Separate opinion of Judge Higgins ["Cour internationale de Justice – International Court of Justice | International Court of Justice"](https://web.archive.org/web/20110112025712/http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1681.pdf) (PDF). Archived from [the original](http://www.icj-cij.org/docket/files/131/1681.pdf) (PDF) on 12 January 2011. Retrieved 7 February 2010.). Paul De Waart said that the Advisory Opinion of the International Court of Justice in 2004 "ascertained the present responsibility of the United Nations to protect Palestine's statehood. It affirmed the applicability of the prohibition of acquisition of Palestinian territory by Israel and confirmed the illegality of the Israeli settlements in the Occupied Palestinian Territory. Moreover, the existence of the Palestinian people as the rightful claimant to the Occupied Palestinian Territory is no longer open to question (See De Waart, Paul J. I. M., "International Court of Justice Firmly Walled in the Law of Power in the Israeli–Palestinian Peace Process", *Leiden Journal of International Law*, 18 (2005), pp. 467–487).

[^cite_note-238]: &#x20;R. Conder, Claude (1877). ["The Moslem Mukams"](https://archive.org/details/quarterlystateme09pale/page/n100/mode/1up?view=theater). *Palestine Exploration Quarterly*. **9** (2): 89–91. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1179/peq.1877.9.2.89](https://doi.org/10.1179%2Fpeq.1877.9.2.89). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0031-0328](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0031-0328). In their religious observances and sanctuaries we find, as in their language, the true history of the country. On a basis of polytheistic faith which most probably dates back to pre-Israelite times, we find a growth of the most heterogeneous description: Christian tradition, Moslem history and foreign worship are mingled so as often to be entirely indistinguishable, and the so-called Moslem is found worshipping at shrines consecrated to Jewish, Samaritan, Christian, and often Pagan memories. It is in worship at these shrines that the religion of the peasantry consists. Moslem by profession, they often spend their lives without entering a mosque, and attach more importance to the favour and protection of the village Mukam than to Allah himself, or to Mohammed his prophet... The reverence shown for these sacred spots is unbounded. Every fallen stone from the building, every withered branch of the tree, is carefully preserved.
